Poroelastic plate model obtained by simultaneous homogenization and dimension reduction
Marin Bužančić, Pedro Hernández-Llanos, Igor Velčić, Josip Žubrinić

Abstract
In this paper, the starting point of our analysis is a coupled system of linear elasticity and Stokes equation. We consider two small parameters: the thickness h h of the thin plate and the pore scale ε ⁡ ( h ) \varepsilon(h) which depends on h h . We will focus specifically on the case when the pore size is comparatively small relative to the thickness of the plate. The main goal here is derive a model of a poroelastic plate, starting from the 3 ​ D 3D problem as h h goes to zero, using simultaneous homogenization and dimension reduction techniques. The obtained model generalizes the poroelastic plate model derived by A. Mikelić et. al. in 2015 using dimension reduction techniques from 3 ​ D 3D Biot’s equations in the sense that it also covers the case of contacts of poroelastic and (poro)elastic plate as well as the evolution equation with inertial term. Key words Homogenization; dimension reduction; poroelastic plate; two-scale convergence 2020 AMS Subject Classification: 35B27; 74F10; 74K20; 74Q15; 76M50



Dedicated to the memory of Andro Mikelić.

1
Introduction

The derivation of Biot’s equations using the homogenization approach is a relatively old topic (see
[
11
]
,
[
12
]
). Recently, there has been growing attention devoted to deriving and studying poroelastic equations in various contexts. We will mention just a few of them. In
[
18
]
, the authors investigate a thin layer of an elastic body immersed in a fluid modeled by Stokes equations. In a recent work
[
19
]
, the author derives the poroelastic plate model in a regime where the size of the pores is on the same scale as the thickness of the body. In
[
21
]
, the quasi-static and evolutionary equations for poroelastic plates are analyzed. In
[
3
]
, the existence result for the interaction between fluid and poroelastic structures is discussed. In
[
5
]
, the authors analyze the quasi-static Biot system of poroelasticity for both compressible and incompressible constituents. In
[
23
]
, the model for the contact between poroelastic and elastic bodies is derived. Finally, in
[
22
]
, the authors derive the model of quasi-static Biot’s plates by performing a dimension reduction of
3D
Biot’s equations.

The current paper focuses on deriving Biot’s plate equations under the condition that the size of the pores, denoted as
\varepsilon(h)
, is significantly smaller than the thickness of the plate, denoted as
h
, that is:
\varepsilon(h)\ll h
.
The starting point is the coupled system consisting of the evolution Stokes equation for incompressible fluid and the linearized equations of elasticity. Utilizing modifications of Griso’s decomposition (referenced in
[
20
]
and
[
30
]
,
[
6
]
,
[
8
]
), we perform simultaneous homogenization and dimension reduction. Griso’s decomposition has previously been employed to obtain the model of homogenized plates within the contexts of both linear and non-linear elasticity (see
[
30
,
6
]
for the case of linear and non-linear elasticity in moderate contrast and
[
8
]
for the case of the elastic plate in high contrast).

In the limit (resulting in the quasi-static case), we derive a generalized version of Biot’s plate model obtained in
[
22
]
. Our derivation also encompasses scenarios involving the contact between elastic and poroelastic plates, as well as interactions between two distinct poroelastic plates. Consequently, the resulting limit equations are more intricate and do not exhibit the same decoupling observed in the equations derived in
[
22
]
, and also analyzed in
[
21
]
.

The derivation of the poroelastic plate model via simultaneous homogenization and dimension reduction also furnishes additional insights into the limit Darcy’s law and appropriate boundary conditions, as well as interface conditions in the case of contacts, establishing their connection with the microscopic model.

There is a notable difference between the quasi-static model obtained in this paper and the one derived in
[
19
]
, where the macroscopic pressure depends only on macroscopic in-plane variables, leading to different limit equations. In this paper (akin to
[
22
]
), the limit equations solely involve the derivative of pressure in the vertical direction. More precisely, the microscopic fluid velocity in the effective Darcy’s law is driven only by the derivative of the pressure in the vertical direction.

One of the challenges encountered in deriving the model was to establish the correct scalings of the constants (i.e. the relation of the elasticity constants with respect to viscosity) which gives Biot’s plate model in the limit, since we have to scale them with respect to two small parameters
\varepsilon
and
h
.

In our model derivation, we also address the scenario resulting in the plate model with inertial terms, thereby providing justification for the model analyzed in
[
21
]
. When considering models with inertial terms, it’s well recognized that the full
3D
macroscopic Biot’s equations exhibit memory effects (see
[
12
]
).
In this context, there exists a certain analogy between poroelastic equations and linear elastic equations with high-contrast inclusions. Namely, to obtain Biot’s equations, viscosity is put in high contrast with elasticity constants. However, the limit plate equations derived here do not exhibit memory effects (see also
[
8
, Section 3.4.1]
). This absence can be attributed to the fact that we derive the model in the bending regime, which is valid for long times (we implicitly scale time for the derivation of evolution equations with inertial terms).
The techniques outlined in this paper could also enable us to derive models in the membrane regime (referenced in
[
8
]
), where it is expected that memory effects for macroscopic equations would be present. However, these equations are degenerate in the vertical component of deformation, thus their significance for the applications is not clear (see
[
8
, Section 3.4.2]
).

For analyzing the limit equations, we adopt the approach of
[
21
]
. Additional effort is required due to the more generalized nature of the equations compared to those analyzed in
[
21
]
. This is particularly true for equations incorporating inertial terms.

Next, we briefly outline the structure of the paper. In Section
2
, we discuss the problem’s setting and provide some
a priori
estimates for fluid-elastic structure interaction. Section
3
is dedicated to deriving and analyzing the quasi-static Biot’s plate model, while Section
4
focuses on deriving and analyzing the Biot’s plate model with inertial term in the bending regime. The Appendix contains auxiliary claims essential for dimension reduction, such as Griso’s decomposition and its consequences, along with auxiliary definitions and claims about two-scale convergence.

The main results of the paper are summarized as follows. In the quasi-static case
Theorem
3.1
provides the compactness result; Theorem
3.7
provides the convergence result, identifying the limit model;
Theorem
3.16
provides uniqueness and existence results and
Theorem
3.22
and Theorem
3.23
provide the strong convergence result.
Similarly in the case with inertial term
Theorem
4.1
provides the compactness result and the limit Biot’s equations;
Theorem
4.3
provides the uniqueness result;
Theorem
4.8
provides the existence result and
Theorem
4.10
provides the strong convergence result.
We provide more details about the results in the next section.

1.1
Techniques and novelties

The limit models for Biot’s plate are derived using simultaneous homogenization and dimension reduction (see Theorem
3.7
and Theorem
4.1
). In the quasi-static case our model extends the model obtained in
[
22
]
(see Remark
3.10
below), obtained by doing dimension reduction from
3D
macroscopic Biot’s equations (adapting the techniques from
[
9
]
). In the case with inertial term this is, to the best of our knowledge, the first justification of such a model.
We emphasize the fact that, starting from the full
3D
Biot’s model with inertial term and doing dimension reduction, it is not a priori clear how the limit plate model would look like, since the full
3D
Biot’s model has memory effects (see
[
12
]
and Remark
4.2
below).
Doing simultaneous homogenization and dimension reduction additionally enables us to discuss influence of microscopic boundary conditions on the limit model and to obtain limit Darcy’s law (see Remark
3.8
and Section
3.6
below).

To obtain the limit model, we adapt the method used in
[
30
,
6
,
8
]
. The method consists in using modification of Griso’s decomposition to obtain the compactness statements in Theorem
3.1
and Theorem
4.1
. It relies on the decomposition of sequences with bounded symmetrized scaled gradients and obtaining the strongest compactness statement for such sequences (the results are recalled and adapted in Appendix
A
). Unlike the approach of
[
9
]
which focuses more on obtaining limit displacement and justifying Kirchoff-Love ansatz, the method we use here focuses more on obtaining appropriate compactness results for sequences with bounded symmetrized scaled gradients, while Kirchoff-Love ansatz is incorporated in these results. In order to obtain such results, we need to use certain estimates valid on
h
level, the information on the limit displacement is not sufficient (see Theorem
A.1
below).

We also obtain the limit model for contacts between different types of poroelastic cells and identify two main types of contacts under which we are able to obtain the limit boundary condition for the pressure at the interface (one of them is Neumann condition and the other is continuity of the pressure). They correspond to two different situations, depending whether there is a flow at the interface or not.
To the best of our knowledge this is the first treatment of such problems in the context of poroelasticity.

The limit models are derived under the minimal regularity assumptions on the loads (the results in
[
12
,
23
,
19
]
require more regularity in time for the loads).
On the microscopic level, we assume that the forces possess
L^{2}
or
H^{1}
regularity in time, depending on whether we want to obtain in the limit quasi-static case or case with inertial term (which is, anyhow, quasi-static in in-plane components).
More precisely, to obtain the quasi-static limit equations, we require boundedness in
H^{1}
in time for the loads, while in the evolution case, boundedness in
H^{1}
in time is only enforced for the in-plane components of the loads and for the vertical component, we impose only
L^{2}
boundedness in time. It’s worth noting that this requirement is less restrictive than the ones in
[
23
]
and
[
19
]
, which require higher regularity. This minimal requirement is exactly the one that is necessary for the existence result of the limit equations.
Consequently, on the microscopic level we have to deal with weak solution defined in
[
15
]
, which doesn’t guarantee that the pressure posses
L^{2}
regularity in time (unless the forces have
H^{1}
regularity in time).
Also, the pressure converges weakly only in
H^{-1}
in time, and an additional regularity of the limit pressure is attained from the limit equations themselves (see the proof of Theorem (
3.7
)).
We also don’t use the condition that the loads at zero moment are zero (cf.
[
12
,
23
,
19
]
) except for the strong convergence results in Theorem
3.22
, Theorem
3.23
and Theorem
4.10
, where it is necessary.

To obtain the existence and uniqueness result for the limit problems we use the approach from
[
21
]
(see Theorem
3.16
and Theorem
4.8
). This approach required additional considerations since our limit equations are more complex than the one analyzed in
[
21
]
(in particular in-plane and vertical components of displacement do not decouple). Moreover, in the case with inertial term we prove uniqueness directly (see Theorem
4.3
, the proof is not given in
[
21
]
) and prove existence for less regular loads by using Galerkin approximation (and relying on semigroup approach as suggested in
[
21
]
), see Remark
3.21
for comparison between semigroup approach and approach via Galerkin approximation.

The strong convergence result is proved in Theorem
3.22
, Theorem
3.23
and Theorem
4.10
(as in
[
12
,
23
]
in the context of
3D
Biot’s equations). This requires to prove certain energy-type equality (see Proposition
3.20
and Proposition
4.9
below), which has to be done by the approximation argument for the solutions that don’t posses enough regularity (as a consequence of less regular loads than the one considered e.g., in
[
12
,
23
]
). This approximation is done via semigroup approach.

1.2
Notation

Throughout this paper, we denote by
\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\rm sym}
the sets of natural, integer, real, the space of real
m\times n
matrices and the space of real
n\times n
symmetric matrices respectively, this two last spaces endowed with the usual Euclidean norm
|\boldsymbol{F}|=\sqrt{\text{tr}\,\boldsymbol{F}^{T}\boldsymbol{F}}
. If
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
, we denote by
\mathbf{A}:\mathbf{B}
the scalar product

\mathbf{A}:\mathbf{B}=\text{tr}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{B}).

For
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
\operatorname{sym}\mathbf{A}
denotes the symmetric part of
\mathbf{A}
.
We denote the coordinate vectors by
\boldsymbol{e}^{i}
,
i=1,2,3
. Unless otherwise stated, the Greek indices
\alpha,\beta
take values in the set
\{1,2\}
. For the vectors
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we denote by
\widehat{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})
the vector containing the first two variables. If the vector valued function
\boldsymbol{u}
is taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we denote the first two components by
\boldsymbol{u}_{*}=(u_{1},u_{2})
.
If
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we denote by
\mathbf{a}\odot\mathbf{b}
the symmetric part of the tensor product
\mathbf{a}\otimes\mathbf{b}
, i.e.
\mathbf{a}\odot\mathbf{b}:=\operatorname{sym}(\mathbf{a}\otimes\mathbf{b})
. If
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, we denote by
\boldsymbol{a}\wedge\boldsymbol{b}
their cross product.

With
\iota:\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
, we denote the natural inclusion

\iota(\mathbf{A})_{\alpha\beta}=\mathbf{A}_{\alpha\beta},\quad\iota(\mathbf{A})_{i3}=\iota(\mathbf{A})_{3i}=0,\quad\alpha,\beta\in\{1,2\},\ i\in\{1,2,3\}.

For
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we denote by
\bar{A}
its closure and by
|A|
its Lebesgue measure. Furthermore,
\chi_{A}
denotes the characteristic function. The open ball of radius
r>0
centered at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
is denoted by
B(x,r)
.

We denote the unit interval by
I=\left(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)
, the unit cell by
Y=[0,1)^{3}
, and the flat torus with quotient topology by
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}^{3}/\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.
For a subset
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{Y}
, we denote by
Y^{\prime}
the associated subset of
Y
through the identification map.
This means that
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}=\{y+{\mathbb{Z}}^{3}:y\in Y^{\prime}\}
. Analogously we define
\widehat{Y}=[0,1]^{2}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Again, for
y\in\mathcal{Y}
,
\hat{y}
denotes
\hat{y}=(y_{1},y_{2})
.

For an open set
A
and
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,
C^{k}(A)
denotes the set of
k
-times continuously differentiable functions on
A
.
If
A
is a closed set, then
C^{k}(A)
is defined as the space of functions that are restrictions of functions in
C^{k}(A^{\prime})
for some open set
A^{\prime}\supset A
.
The space
C_{c}^{k}(A)
denotes the set of
k
-times differentiable functions with compact support in
A
. The space
C^{k}(\mathcal{Y})
denotes the set of
k
-times differentiable functions on the torus, while
H^{k}(\mathcal{Y})
denotes the closure of
C^{k}(\mathcal{Y})
in the
H^{k}
Sobolev norm.
If
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a rectangle (i.e.
\omega=(a,b)\times(c,d)
), then the set
C^{k}_{\#}(\omega)
is the set of
k
-times continuously differentiable functions with bounded derivatives that can be extended to periodic functions in
C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, with period
\omega
.
In addition, for an open subset

B\subset\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
C^{k}_{\#}(\omega\times B)
the set of
k
-times differentiable functions on
\omega\times B
that can be extended to functions in
C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times B)
which are periodic in
(x_{1},x_{2})
-variables with period
\omega
. The spaces
H^{k}_{\#}(\omega)
and
H^{k}_{\#}(\omega\times B)
are the closures of sets
C^{k}_{\#}(\omega)
and
C^{k}_{\#}(\omega\times B)
in
H^{k}
norm, respectively.
The set
\dot{C}^{k}(A)
denotes the subspace of functions in
C^{k}(A)
with zero mean.
In an analogous way we define the spaces
\dot{C}_{c}^{k}(A)
,
\dot{H}^{k}(A)
,
\dot{H}_{\#}^{k}(\omega)

\dot{H}^{k}_{\#}(\omega\times B)
,
B\subset{\mathbb{R}}
. These definitions of functional spaces are naturally extended for spaces of functions taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
In general,
\rightarrow
denotes the strong convergence and
\rightharpoonup
denotes the weak (or weak-*) convergence.

For
A\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
f\in L^{1}(A)
,
\fint_{A}f
denotes the mean value of
f
on the set
A
.
For
f\in L^{1}(A\times I)
,
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we denote by
\bar{f}\in L^{1}(A)
the function

\bar{f}=\int_{I}f\,dx_{3},

(1.1)

and if
f\in L^{1}(Y)
, we denote by

\langle f\rangle_{Y}:=\int_{Y}f\,dy.

(1.2)

For a smooth function
f
, its support is denoted by
\textrm{supp}f
.

For a Banach space
V
, we denote its dual by
V^{\prime}
.
If
f\in V^{\prime}
and
v\in V
, then
{}_{V^{\prime}}\langle f,v\rangle_{V}
denotes the value
f(v)
.
The set of continuous linear operators from vector space
V
to vector space
W
is denoted by
L(V,W)
.
Given an operator
\mathcal{A}
, we denote by
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
and
\textrm{Ker}(\mathcal{A})
its domain and kernel.
For
h>0
, we denote by
\pi_{h}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
the mapping

\pi_{h}(\mathbf{a})=(ha_{1},ha_{2},a_{3}).

(1.3)

For a function
\boldsymbol{u}:\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we denote its symmetric gradient by
e(\boldsymbol{u}):=\operatorname{sym}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}
. In the analogous way, we define
e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{u})
and
e_{y}(\boldsymbol{u})
for the mappings
\boldsymbol{u}:\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\boldsymbol{u}:Y(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, respectively.
For functions
\boldsymbol{u}:\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
h>0
we denote by
\nabla_{h}
the scaled gradient

\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}:=\left(\partial_{1}\boldsymbol{u},\partial_{2}\boldsymbol{u},\frac{1}{h}\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{u}\right).

(1.4)

If
\boldsymbol{u}
is taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we denote the scaled divergence and the symmetric scaled gradient by

\operatorname{div}_{h}\boldsymbol{u}:=\text{tr}\,\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u},\quad e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\operatorname{sym}\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}.

(1.5)

Throughout Section
2
, Section
3
and Section
4
we consider
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
a rectangle with vertices having integer coordinates, and assume that there exist
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that
h=\frac{1}{k}
and
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{kl}
.
We will write
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(h)
and assume in Section
3
and Section
4
that
\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\varepsilon(h)}{h}=0
.

We define
\Omega^{h}=\omega\times(hI)
for
h>0
, and
\Omega=\omega\times I
.

2
Setting of the problem

In this section, we present the microscopic problem. In Section
2.1
, we discuss the geometry of the microscopic problem. In Section
2.2
, we provide the equations of the microscopic problem and establish the existence result. Finally, in Section
2.3
, we derive some
a priori
estimates necessary to obtain the limit equations.

2.1
Geometry and material assumptions

2.1.1
The poroelastic cells and interface conditions

To extend our analysis to encompass interactions between various types of poroelastic plates, including interactions between poroelastic and elastic plates, we permit our material to consist of diverse poroelastic materials.
To this end, we assume the existence of a finite number of typical cells consisting of fluid and solid part. In other words, we assume that there exist
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and pairs
(\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i},\mathcal{Y}_{s}^{i})
,
i=1,\dots,m
of open subsets of
\mathcal{Y}
with Lipschitz boundary such that
(see Figure
1
)

\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{s}^{i}=\emptyset,\quad\overline{\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}}\cup\overline{\mathcal{Y}_{s}^{i}}=\mathcal{Y},\quad i=1,\dots,m,

i.e. there exist pairs
({Y}_{f}^{i},{Y}_{s}^{i})
1
1

1

Recall, we assume that
Y_{f}^{i}
,
Y_{s}^{i}
are associated sets of
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{s}^{i}
through the identification map (
recall Section
1.2
). Note that the closures of
Y_{f}^{i}
i.e.,
Y_{s}^{i}
(in the topology of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
) don’t need to have opposite boundaries matching. This happens if
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}
(i.e.
\mathcal{Y}_{s}^{i}
) has part of its boundary (in the torus topology) on
y_{j}=0
,
j=1,2,3
, see Figure
3
(b).
,
i=1,\dots,m
, of open sets of
Y
(in the relative topology) with Lipschitz boundary, such that

{Y}_{f}^{i}\cap{Y}_{s}^{i}=\emptyset,\quad\overline{{Y}_{f}^{i}}\cup\overline{{Y}_{s}^{i}}={Y},\quad i=1,\dots,m.

We also assume that both
Y_{f}^{i}
and
Y_{s}^{i}
(consequently, both
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{s}^{i}
) are connected sets.

Figure 1
:
Illustration of the poroelastic plate, i.e. the fluid and the solid phases

The set
\Omega^{h}
is partitioned into
\widetilde{\Omega}_{s}^{h}
and
\widetilde{\Omega}_{f}^{h}
, representing the regions occupied by solid and fluid, respectively. For each
h>0
, these sets are defined in the following way. Let
i_{h}:\mathbb{Z}^{3}\to\{1,\dots,m\}
be a given map, which selects and associates a typical cell type to every index
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{3}
.
We set

\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f/s}=\bigcup_{k}\varepsilon\,(k+Y_{f/s}^{i_{h}(k)}),

(2.1)

where the union is taken over all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
such that
\varepsilon(k+Y)\subset\Omega
.
Note that the dependence on
\varepsilon
is concealed as a dependence on
h
(since
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(h)
), and will remain so throughout the paper.
We assume that both
\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{s}
and
\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f}
are Lipschitz and that
\widetilde{\Omega}_{s}^{h}
is also connected.
We denote the interface between the two phases with
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{h}=\partial\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f}\cap\partial\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{s}
. In addition, we will assume that the fluid domain does not intersect the upper and lower boundaries, i.e.

\partial\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f}\cap\left(\{x_{3}=-h/2\}\cup\{x_{3}=h/2\}\right)=\emptyset.

(2.2)

We discuss the consequences of dropping this condition in Section
3.6.4
below.
Furthermore, we introduce sets
\Omega^{h}_{f/s}\subset\Omega
as the images of
\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f/s}
through the rescaling

(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=(x_{1}^{h},x_{2}^{h},h^{-1}x_{3}^{h}),\quad(x^{h}_{1},x^{h}_{2},x^{h}_{3})\in\Omega^{h}.

(2.3)

Additionally we assume layer-like structure of our composite poroelastic material.
Thus, we impose the following compatibility condition on the limiting structure:

\left|\chi_{\Omega^{h}_{f/s}}(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\chi_{U_{i}}(x)\chi_{\mathcal{Y}^{i}_{f/s}}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\right|\to 0\textrm{ almost everywhere in }\Omega,

(2.4)

where
U_{1}=\omega\times I_{1},\dots,U_{m}=\omega\times I_{m}\subset\Omega

denote the layers of the material. Here
I_{1},\dots I_{m}

are disjoint, open, non-empty sub-intervals of
I
of the form:

I_{i}=(d_{i},d_{i+1}),\quad i=1,\dots,m,\quad-\frac{1}{2}=d_{1}<\dots<d_{m+1}=\frac{1}{2}.

(2.5)

Remark 2.1
.

In other words, the sets
U_{1},\dots U_{m}
model regions in
\Omega
occupied by a poroelastic material with cell type
\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{f/s},\dots,\mathcal{Y}^{m}_{f/s}
, respectively.
We allow that some of
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,m
, are empty (meaning that the region
U_{i}
is purely elastic).
By the same analysis presented in this paper, it is also possible to consider more general types of regions than the ones defined here (see Section
3.6
), which we don’t analyze for the simplicity of the exposure.

However, for the derivation of the model we will need more restrictive assumption than (
2.4
).
Namely, we will impose layer–like structure for every
h
.

Before stating the required assumption, we introduce the following notation.
For a subset
S\subset Y
we denote by
\widetilde{S}_{h}^{\#}
the set

\widetilde{S}_{h}^{\#}:=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}\varepsilon\,(k+S),

while by
S_{h}^{\#}
we denote the image of
\widetilde{S}_{h}^{\#}
through the rescaling (
2.3
).

Assumption 2.2
.

For every
h
there exists numbers
(d_{i}^{h})_{i=1,\dots,m+1}
, such that

-\frac{1}{2}=d_{1}^{h}<\dots<d_{m+1}^{h}=\frac{1}{2},

(2.6)

and we have

\Omega^{h}_{f/s}\cap(\omega\times I_{i}^{h})=\left(Y_{f/s}^{i}\right)_{h}^{\#}\cap(\omega\times I_{i}^{h}),\quad\forall h<h_{0},\quad i=1,\dots,m,

where

I_{i}^{h}=(d_{i}^{h},d_{i+1}^{h}),\quad i=1,\dots,m.

The above assumption tells us that whole region
\omega\times I_{i}^{h}
consists only of poroelastic cells of type
i
. As a consequence of (
2.4
) we have that

d_{i}^{h}\to d_{i},\textrm{ as }h\to 0,\quad i=1,\dots,m+1.

(2.7)

We distinguish two different type of contacts between the regions (see also Figure
3
):
More precisely, if for
i\in{1,\dots,m-1}
we have that
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i+1}\cap\{y_{3}=0\}=\emptyset
, then we say that there is
no flow at the interface
.
2
2

2

Strictly speaking the set
\{y_{3}=0\}
, looked as the subset of
\mathcal{Y}
, is actually the set
\{y+{\mathbb{Z}}^{3}:y_{3}=0\}.
On the other hand,
if for
i\in{1,\dots,m-1}
we have that
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i+1}\cap\{y_{3}=0\}\neq\emptyset
, then we say that there is
a flow at the interface
.
3
3

3

Note that, since both
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i+1}
are open, there is actually a ball contained in
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i+1}
whose center belongs to the plane
y_{3}=0
.

Figure 3
:
(a) is an example of interface where there is no flow, while (b) is an example of interface such that there is a flow. Note also that the cells in (b) don’t have the opposite boundaries matching.

(a)

(b)

Figure 4
:
In this figure we explain poroelastic region and the domain of vertical permeability.
For simplicity of the picture we drew that each
I_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,8
has only one (layer of) cell.
Here

J_{p}=J_{2}\cup J_{3}\cup J_{4}\cup J_{5}
and
J_{K}=J_{2}\cup J_{3}\cup J_{4}
and
J_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,5
are assumed to be open intervals.

2.1.2
The poroelastic region and the domain of vertical permeability

Next we wish to emphasize the crucial part of the domain on which pressure is not zero.
This set (which we denote by
\Omega_{p}
) is obtained by excluding from
\Omega
layers of purely elastic medium.
More precisely,
if there is a flow on the interface between
U_{i}
and
U_{i+1}
, we join them into one region (including their interface).
After doing this procedure for every
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
we obtain the regions
V_{1},\dots,V_{s}
,
s\leq m
, of the form
V_{i}=\omega\times J_{i}
, for
j=1,\dots,s
.
Here
J_{i}
is an open, non-empty sub-interval of
I
that is a union of two or more neighbouring intervals from the family
\{I_{1},\dots,I_{m}\}
including their endpoints (except the left endpoint of the first interval and the right endpoint of the last interval in the union).
We then define

\Omega_{p}:=\omega\times J_{p},\quad J_{p}:=\bigcup_{\begin{matrix}\scriptstyle i\in\{1,\dots,s\}\\[-3.01389pt]
\scriptstyle V_{i}\textrm{ not purely elastic}\end{matrix}}J_{i}.

(2.8)

We refer to
\Omega_{p}
as the poroelastic region (see Figure
4
).

Furthermore, we will distinguish layers of
\Omega_{p}
with cells which allow the fluid flow in
x_{3}
-direction.
We will denote this subset as
\Omega_{K}
, and refer to it as the domain of vertical permeability.
In order to do this, first we define the set
J_{K}\subset J_{p}
by excluding those
I_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m
, for which we have that
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}\cap\{y_{3}=0\}=\emptyset
, i.e.

J_{K}:=J_{p}\backslash\bigcup_{\begin{matrix}\scriptstyle i\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\[-3.01389pt]
\scriptstyle\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}\cap\{y_{3}=0\}=\emptyset\end{matrix}}I_{i}

(2.9)

and we define

\Omega_{K}:=\omega\times J_{K}.

As we will see below,
\Omega_{p}
is the region where the coefficient
M_{0}
, appearing in the limit equations, is uniformly strictly greater than zero, while
\Omega_{K}\subset\Omega_{p}
is the region where the coefficient
\mathbb{K}_{33}
is strictly greater than zero (both coefficients
M_{0}
and
\mathbb{K}_{33}
are defined in Section
3.2
below). On the region
\Omega\backslash\Omega_{p}
both coefficients are equal to zero and furthermore,
\mathbb{K}_{33}
is equal to zero on
\Omega_{p}\backslash\Omega_{K}
(see Proposition
3.3
). The importance of uniform positivity of these coefficients is seen in Section
3.4
.

Remark 2.3
.

Note that
\Omega\backslash\Omega_{p}
(i.e.
\Omega\backslash\Omega_{K}
) might have an isolated set of the form
\omega\times\{d_{i}\}
, for some
i=2,\dots,m
, as its subset (see again Figure
4
). For this reason
\Omega_{p}
might not be Lipschitz domain.
Also, the functions belonging to
H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega_{p})
might have a jump in the trace on the part of the boundary of the form
\omega\times\{d_{i}\}
. Consequently, the space
C_{\#}^{1}(\bar{\Omega}_{p})
is not dense in
H_{\#}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
. However, the functions in
C_{\#}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
, which together with their derivatives belong to
L^{\infty}(\Omega_{p})
, are dense in
H_{\#}^{1}(\Omega_{p})
.
The space
H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega_{p})
can be easily understood, since we have

H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega_{p})=\bigoplus_{\begin{matrix}\scriptstyle i\in\{1,\dots,s\}\\[-3.01389pt]
\scriptstyle V_{i}\textrm{ not purely elastic}\end{matrix}}H_{\#}^{1}(V_{i}).

The analogous observations are valid for the set
J_{p}
, i.e.
H^{1}(J_{p})
(also for
\Omega_{K},J_{K}
and
H_{\#}^{1}(\Omega_{K}),H^{1}(J_{K})
). For the consequence of these observations on the limit problem see Remark
3.9
.

2.1.3
The elasticity tensor

The elastic properties of the material are modeled with elasticity tensors
\mathbb{A}^{1},\dots,\mathbb{A}^{m}
that satisfy

\exists\nu>0\textrm{ such that }\nu|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2}\leq\mathbb{A}^{i}(y)\boldsymbol{\xi}:\boldsymbol{\xi}\leq\nu^{-1}|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}_{\text{sym}},\quad y\in Y,i=1,\dots,m.

(2.10)

We assume that the following standard symmetries hold

\mathbb{A}^{s}_{ijkl}(y)=\mathbb{A}^{s}_{jikl}(y)=\mathbb{A}^{s}(y)_{klij},\quad y\in Y,i,j,k,l\in\{1,2,3\},\quad s=1,\dots,m.

(2.11)

Finally, we define

\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{h}(x^{h})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3},\,\varepsilon(k+Y)\subset\Omega^{h}}\mathbb{A}^{i_{h}(k)}(x^{h}/\varepsilon-k)\chi_{\varepsilon(k+Y_{s}^{i_{h}(k)})}(x^{h}),\quad x^{h}\in\Omega^{h}.

As a consequence of (
2.4
), i.e. Assumption
2.2
, we have

\left|\mathbb{A}^{h}(x)-\mathbb{A}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{3}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\right|\to 0,\textrm{ almost everywhere in }\Omega,

(2.12)

where

\mathbb{A}^{h}(x):=\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{h}(x_{1},x_{2},hx_{3})
and

\mathbb{A}(x_{3},y):=\chi_{I_{1}}(x_{3})\mathbb{A}^{1}(y)\chi_{\mathcal{Y}_{s}^{1}}(y)+\cdots+\chi_{I_{m}}(x_{3})\mathbb{A}^{m}(y)\chi_{\mathcal{Y}_{s}^{m}}(y).

(2.13)

Note that
For
x_{3}\in I_{i}
, we define
\mathcal{Y}_{f/s}(x_{3}):=\mathcal{Y}_{f/s}^{i}
, and for
x_{3}=d_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m+1
,
\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3}):=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3}):=\mathcal{Y}
.
We also define the set

\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}_{f/s}^{x_{3}}:=\{(x,y)\in\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}:y\in\mathcal{Y}_{f/s}(x_{3})\}.

(2.14)

All the functions on
L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}_{f/s}^{x_{3}})
we can naturally extend by zero and consider them as functions in
L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y})
(
\equiv L^{2}(\Omega\times Y)
).
For given
x_{3}\in I
and
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{Y}
the space
H^{1}(\mathcal{C})
is defined as the set of restrictions of functions belonging to
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y})
on
\mathcal{C}
.
The set
H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{C})
is understood as the set of functions which, when extended by zero outside
\mathcal{C}
, belong to
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y})
.

2.2
The microscopic equations

We consider the following fluid-solid-structure interaction problem on
\Omega^{h}
which couples Stokes equation with linearized elasticity:

\displaystyle\eta\widetilde{\kappa}^{h}_{f}\partial_{tt}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}+\frac{\nabla\widetilde{p}^{h}}{h^{2}}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\Delta\partial_{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}^{h},\quad\displaystyle\text{div}\,\partial_{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}=0\quad\text{in}\,\,\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\eta\widetilde{\kappa}_{s}^{h}\partial_{tt}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}=\frac{1}{h^{2}}\text{div}\,(\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{h}e(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}))+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}^{h}\quad\text{in}\,\,\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{s},

(2.16)

\widetilde{\sigma}^{f,h}:=-\frac{1}{h^{2}}\widetilde{p}^{h}I+2\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}e\left(\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}\right)\quad\text{in}\,\,\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f},\quad\quad\widetilde{\sigma}^{s,h}:=\frac{1}{h^{2}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{h}e(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h})\quad\text{in}\,\,\widetilde{\Omega}_{s}^{h},

(2.17)

\displaystyle[\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}]=0,\quad\widetilde{\sigma}^{f,h}\boldsymbol{n}_{f}=\widetilde{\sigma}^{s,h}\boldsymbol{n}_{s}\quad\text{on}\,\,\widetilde{\Gamma}^{h},\quad\quad\widetilde{\sigma}^{s,h}\boldsymbol{n}=0\quad\text{on}\,\,\{x_{3}=h/2\}\cup\{x_{3}=-h/2\},

(2.18)

\left.\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}\right|_{\{t=0\}}=\left.\partial_{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}\right|_{\{t=0\}}=0\quad\text{on}\,\,\Omega^{h},\quad\quad\left\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h},\widetilde{p}^{h}\right\}\quad\text{is $\omega$-periodic in }\,(x_{1},x_{2}).

(2.19)

Here,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}|_{\Omega_{s}^{h}}
represents the deformation in the solid, while
\partial_{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}|_{\Omega_{f}^{h}}
denotes the fluid velocity. The function
\widetilde{p}^{h}
denotes pressure, and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}^{h}
represents volume forces. The parameters
\widetilde{\kappa}_{f}^{h}
and
\widetilde{\kappa}_{s}^{h}
denote the fluid and solid densities, respectively, which are positive functions (for the analysis below they don’t have to be constants) bounded from below and above by a positive constant .

The parameter
\eta=\eta(h)>0
serves as a time-scaling parameter. One scenario we analyze is when
\lim_{h\to 0}\eta(h)=0
, which results in the quasi-static regime. The other scenario is
\eta(h)=1
, leading to the bending regime with inertial term.
In Section
2.3
we will prove
a priori
estimates and see how these two regimes influence them. Later, in Section
3
and Section
4
we will analyze these two regimes separately, In Section
3
we will analyze the regime
\lim_{h\to 0}\eta(h)=0
, while in Section
4
we will analyze the case
\eta(h)=1
.

The tensors
\widetilde{\sigma}^{f,h}
and
\widetilde{\sigma}^{s,h}
, given by (
2.17
), represent the stresses in the fluid and solid, respectively. The vector
\boldsymbol{n}
in the second and third equation of (
2.18
) denotes the unit normal at the interface point, from different sides of the interface (in the second equation) or at the upper or lower boundary point (in the third equation).

The equations (
2.15
) are Stokes equations for incompressible fluid, while (
2.16
) is the equation of linearized elasticity (with the appropriate scaling of the constants applied to all of them). The first equation in (
2.18
) expresses the continuity of the deformation at the interface (here
[\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}]
denotes the jump of the function, i.e. the difference of traces from two sides of the interface), while the second equation in (
2.18
) represents the continuity of stresses in the normal direction. The third equation in (
2.18
) is the Neumann boundary condition at the upper and lower boundary (this can be modified by adding surface loads, see Section
3.6
), while the first equation in (
2.19
) represents the zero initial condition (our analysis also accommodates other initial conditions). For simplicity, periodic boundary conditions are imposed on the transverse boundary by the second expression in (
2.19
). Different initial and boundary conditions are discussed in Section
3.6
.

Remark 2.4
.

It may not be immediately evident what is the motivation behind the particular scaling of coefficients in (
2.15
)-(
2.17
), with respect to
\varepsilon
and
h
. The main feature of this chosen scaling is that it leads to the Biot’s plate model as
h\to 0
. One way to interpret it is to consider it as a scaling of viscosity and time (or density) variables (to see this, one has to multiply equations (
2.15
) and (
2.16
) by
h^{2}
).

2.2.1
The weak formulation on the physical domain

The functional space for (
2.15
)-(
2.19
) is the following:

\widetilde{V}^{h}:=\left\{\psi\in H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}):\operatorname{div}\psi=0\textrm{ in }\widetilde{\Omega}_{f}^{h}\right\}.

(2.20)

The variational formulation which corresponds to (
2.15
)-(
2.19
) is given by:

For
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}^{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
find
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\widetilde{V}^{h})
such that
\partial_{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H_{\#}^{1}(\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
and
\partial_{tt}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;(\widetilde{V}^{h})^{\prime})
and

\begin{split}&\eta\widetilde{\kappa}^{h}{}_{(\widetilde{V}^{h})^{\prime}}\langle\partial_{tt}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\rangle_{\widetilde{V}^{h}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f}}2e(\partial_{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}(t)):e(\boldsymbol{v})\,dx^{h}+\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}_{s}^{h}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{h}(x^{h})e(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}(t)):e(\boldsymbol{v})\,dx^{h}\\
&=\int_{\Omega^{h}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}^{h}\boldsymbol{v}\,dx^{h},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\widetilde{V}^{h},\quad\text{a.e.}\,\,\text{in}\,\,(0,T),\end{split}

(2.21)

where

\widetilde{\kappa}^{h}=\widetilde{\kappa}^{h}_{f}\chi_{\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f}}+\widetilde{\kappa}^{h}_{s}\chi_{\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{s}}.

(2.22)

For the analysis below it is enough to assume that
\widetilde{\kappa}^{h}
is a function bounded from below and above by a positive constant. The variational formulation is supplemented with initial conditions (
2.19
).
The existence and uniqueness of the solution of the above problem is given in
[
15
, Theorem 2.4]
.

Remark 2.5
.

Here we discuss the existence and uniqueness of the pressure variable for the problem (
2.21
) (cf. (
2.15
)) .
Classically, as in (
2.21
), weak formulation can be written without pressure with divergence free test functions. Pressure then can be introduced as a Lagrange multiplier and it is important to establish its existence, uniqueness and regularity (see, e.g.
[
17
, Section 5]
for Stokes equation).
From the mathematical point of view, introducing the pressure as a variable enables us to write weak formulation for arbitrary test functions and to write the strong formulation. To this end,
we introduce:

\widetilde{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f}}:=\left\{\varphi\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f})):\varphi(T)=0\right\}.

(2.23)

By integrating (
2.21
) over
[0,t]
, for
t\in[0,T]
, we can introduce the function
\widetilde{P}^{h}(t)
:

\begin{split}&-\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f}}\widetilde{P}^{h}(t)\operatorname{div}\boldsymbol{v}\,dx^{h}=-\eta\int_{\Omega^{h}}\widetilde{\kappa}^{h}\partial_{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}(t)\boldsymbol{v}\,dx^{h}-\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f}}2e(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}(t)):e(\boldsymbol{v})\,dx^{h}\\
&-\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}_{s}^{h}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{h}(x^{h})e\left(\int_{0}^{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}(\tau)\,d\tau\right):e(\boldsymbol{v})\,dx^{h}+\int_{\Omega^{h}}\int_{0}^{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}^{h}(\tau)\,d\tau\boldsymbol{v}\,dx^{h},\quad\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}).\end{split}

(2.24)

By using the same approach as in
[
15
, Section 3.2]
, we can conclude the existence of
\widetilde{P}^{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f}))
, which satisfies (
2.24
), provided that we have the existence of the solution of (
2.21
). Note that (
2.24
) is simply the integrated (in time) variant of (
2.21
) with arbitrary test functions and with ”pressure” variable (actually
\widetilde{P}^{h}
is the integral in time of physical pressure, see below, and consequently has more regularity in time, namely
L^{2}
). This integration in time was done to obtain the existence of
\widetilde{P}^{h}
, by using the results of
[
15
, Section 3.2]
.
In order to come to version of (
2.21
) with pressure variable we need to do the derivation in time of (
2.24
). This is done in the following way: One tests (
2.24
) with
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}(t)
, where
\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}(0,T;H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
is such that
\boldsymbol{v}(T)=0
, and integrates over
[0,T]
.
By doing integration by parts in every term on the right hand side except the first one, and considering (
2.21
), we have that for some
\widetilde{p}^{h}\in(\widetilde{H^{1}_{T}L^{2}_{f}})^{\prime}
the following is satisfied:

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\eta\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{h}}\widetilde{\kappa}^{h}\partial_{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}\,dt\,dx^{h}+\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f}}2e(\partial_{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}):e(\boldsymbol{v})\,dx^{h}\,dt

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}_{s}^{h}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{h}(x^{h})e(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}):e(\boldsymbol{v})\,dx^{h}\,dt-\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}{}_{(\widetilde{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f}})^{\prime}}\langle\widetilde{p}^{h},\operatorname{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}\rangle_{\widetilde{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{h}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}^{h}\boldsymbol{v}\,dx^{h}\,dt,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}(0,T;H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}))\textrm{ such that }\boldsymbol{v}(T)=0.

(2.25)

Here:

_{(\widetilde{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})^{\prime}}\langle\widetilde{p}^{h},\varphi\rangle_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}:=-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f}}\widetilde{P}^{h}\,\partial_{t}\varphi\,dx^{h}\,dt,\quad\forall\varphi\in\widetilde{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f}}.

(2.26)

We write formally
\widetilde{P}^{h}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\widetilde{p}^{h}(\tau)\,d\tau.

This simple observation and the existence of this irregular pressure, under the condition that loads have only
L^{2}
regularity in time, is not discussed in
[
15
]
.
The authors of
[
15
]
obtain the existence of the solution of the problem (
2.27
) below, by using only weak formulation with divergence free (on fluid part) test functions.
However, the existence of
L^{2}
regular in time pressure for the loads that have
H^{1}
regularity in time is obtained in
[
15
]
. It is again done by eliminating the divergence free condition in the space of test functions (i.e., from geometrical point of view by introducing the Lagrange multiplier).

2.2.2
The weak formulation on the fixed domain

In order to work in a fixed domain
\Omega
, we apply the change of variables (
2.3
)
and we define
\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(x):=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}(x^{h})
. In the analogous way we define
p^{h}
,
\boldsymbol{F}^{h}
,
\kappa^{h}
(recall (
2.22
)).
After doing this transformation we obtain the following problem: For
\boldsymbol{F}^{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
find
{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;{V}^{h})
such that
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H_{\#}^{1}({\Omega}^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
,
\partial_{tt}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;({V}^{h})^{\prime})
and
p^{h}\in(H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f})^{\prime}
satisfy (recall (
1.5
))

\begin{split}&\displaystyle-\eta\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\kappa^{h}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}\,dx\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}2e_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}):e_{h}(\boldsymbol{v})\,dx\,dt+\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{s}^{h}}\mathbb{A}^{h}(x)e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}):e_{h}(\boldsymbol{v})\,dx\,dt\\
&-\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}{}_{(H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f})^{\prime}}\langle p^{h},\operatorname{div}_{h}\,\boldsymbol{v}\rangle_{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}\boldsymbol{v}\,dx\,dt,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}(0,T;H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}))\textrm{ such that }\boldsymbol{v}(T)=0.\end{split}

(2.27)

The initial condition is given with

\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(0)=0\quad\text{on}\,\,\Omega.

(2.28)

Here we denoted by
\Omega^{h}_{f/s}
the rescaled
\widetilde{\Omega}^{h}_{f/s}
.
The space
V^{h}
is defined in the following way

V^{h}:=\left\{\psi\in H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}):\operatorname{div}_{h}\psi=0\textrm{ in }\Omega_{f}^{h}\right\}

(2.29)

and the space
H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f}
as

H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f}:=\left\{\varphi\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{f}^{h})):\varphi(T)=0\right\}.

(2.30)

We also define
\Gamma^{h}
as rescaled
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{h}
.

Next we discuss the existence result for the problem (
2.27
) -(
2.28
).
The following result is the consequence of
[
15
, Theorem 2.4, Theorem 3.2, Theorem 3.4]
and Remark
2.5
.

Proposition 2.6
.

1.

Let us suppose that
\boldsymbol{F}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))
. Then,
3D
problem given in (
2.27
) has a unique solution
(\boldsymbol{u}^{h},p^{h})
such that
{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;{V}^{h})
and
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H_{\#}^{1}({\Omega}^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
,
\partial_{tt}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;({V}^{h})^{\prime})
and
p^{h}\in(H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f})^{\prime}
. It additionally satisfies
4
4

4

The expression
t\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}p^{h}(\tau)\,d\tau
is, as explained in Remark
2.5
, understood in the symbolic sense.

\displaystyle\|\partial_{tt}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;(V^{h})^{\prime})}+\|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{f}^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}))}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)\|_{H^{1}(\Omega^{h}_{s};\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\hskip 34.44434pt+\|p^{h}\|_{(H_{T}^{1}L^{2})^{\prime}}+\left\lVert\int_{0}^{\cdot}p^{h}(\tau)\,d\tau\right\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f}))}\leq C(h,\Omega)e^{C(h,\Omega)T}\|\boldsymbol{F}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))},

for some
C(h,\Omega)>0
.

2.

Let us suppose that
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))
. Then, the unique solution of 1. satisfies
(\boldsymbol{u}^{h},p^{h})\in\left(H^{1}(0,T;V^{h})\cap H^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))\right)\times L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f}))
. It additionally satisfies:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\partial_{tt}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\partial_{tt}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{f}^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}))}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{s}^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\hskip 43.05542pt+\|p^{h}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{f}^{h}))}\leq C(h,\Omega)e^{C(h,\Omega)T}\|\boldsymbol{F}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))},

for some
C(h,\Omega)>0
.

Remark 2.7
.

In the case when
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))
we have that the solution of (
2.27
) satisfies the following equality pointwise in time

\begin{split}\displaystyle\eta\int_{\Omega}\kappa^{h}&\partial_{tt}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)\boldsymbol{v}\,dx+\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}2e_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)):e_{h}(\boldsymbol{v})\,dx+\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\Omega_{s}^{h}}\mathbb{A}^{h}(x)e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)):e_{h}(\boldsymbol{v})\,dx\\
&-\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}p^{h}\operatorname{div}_{h}\,\boldsymbol{v}\,dx=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}\boldsymbol{v}\,dx,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\text{a.e.}\,\,\text{in}\,\,(0,T),\end{split}

(2.31)

with initial conditions

\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(0)=\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(0)=0.

This is the consequence of additional regularity of the solution in time given by Proposition
2.6
2.

2.3
A priori
estimates

Here we provide
a priori
estimates for the solutions to the problem (
2.27
) regardless of the assumptions on the time-scaling parameter
\eta=\eta(h)
. We define
\kappa\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
as weak star limit of
\kappa^{h}
. It is a positive function bounded from below and above.
\bar{\kappa}
is then defined according to (
1.1
).

2.3.1
A priori estimates for the displacement

The main result in this section is given in Proposition
2.11
. However, before stating it, we give some auxiliary claims.
First, we introduce the following spaces:

\displaystyle V(\Omega^{h}_{f})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1}(\Omega_{f}^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}):\varphi=0\,\text{ on }\,\,\Gamma^{h}\,\text{and}\,\boldsymbol{\varphi}\,\text{ is $\omega$-periodic in}\,(x_{1},x_{2})\right\},

\displaystyle V(\Omega^{h}_{s})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1}(\Omega_{s}^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}):\varphi\,\text{is $\omega$-periodic in}\,(x_{1},x_{2})\right\}.

We give two helpful lemmas.

Lemma 2.8
.

There exists
C>0
such that for every
h>0
there exists a linear extension operator from
V(\Omega^{h}_{s})
to
H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
(
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\mapsto\widehat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
) that satisfies

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega^{h}_{s};\mathbb{R}^{3})},

\displaystyle\|e_{h}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{s}^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in V(\Omega^{h}_{s}).

Proof.

See
[
24
, Chapter 4]
.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in V(\Omega^{h}_{f})
. Then we have

\|\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C\varepsilon\|\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C\varepsilon\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}.

(2.32)

Proof.

The estimate can be established on each small cube contained in
\Omega
by rescaling it on the physical domain. ∎

The following proposition yields important Korn-type estimate with respect to the fluid and solid component of the symmetric gradient.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in H^{1}\left(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)
such that
\boldsymbol{\xi}(0)=0
and
\int_{\Omega}\kappa^{h}\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\,dx=0
, for all
t\in[0,T]
.
Then the following estimate holds for all
t\in[0,T]
, with a constant
C
independent of
h
(recall
1.3
),

\|\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq\displaystyle C\left\{\frac{1}{h}\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{s};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\frac{\varepsilon}{h}\int_{0}^{t}\|e_{h}(\partial_{\tau}\boldsymbol{\xi}(\tau))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\,d\tau\right\}.

(2.33)

Proof.

For every
t\in[0,T]
, let
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t)
be the extension of
\left.\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\right|_{\Omega^{h}_{s}}
, given by Lemma
2.8
. We define
\boldsymbol{z}(t)=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)
on
\Omega^{h}_{f}
and zero elsewhere. Then for every
t\in(0,T)
we have
\boldsymbol{z}(t)\in V(\Omega^{h}_{f})
. As a consequence of Lemma
2.9
we have

\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C\varepsilon\|\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C\varepsilon\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{z}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})},

(2.34)

for all
t\in(0,T)
. We conclude

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C\varepsilon\left\{\|e_{h}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\right\}.

(2.35)

Next, from (
2.35
) and using the fact that
\varepsilon,h\leq 1
and Proposition
A.7
we obtain

\displaystyle\|\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq\|\pi_{1/h}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\pi_{1/h}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\xi}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\pi_{1/h}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{1}{h}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\pi_{1/h}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{C\varepsilon}{h}\left\{\|e_{h}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigg\{\frac{1}{h}\|e_{h}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\left|\int_{\Omega}\pi_{1/h}(\kappa^{h}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t))\right|+\frac{\varepsilon}{h}\|e_{h}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}

\displaystyle\hskip 21.52771pt+\frac{\varepsilon}{h}\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\Bigg\}

(2.36)

Note that since
\int_{\Omega}\kappa^{h}\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\,dx=0
, we have that

\int_{\Omega}\pi_{1/h}(\kappa^{h}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t)))\,dx=\int_{\Omega}\pi_{1/h}(\kappa^{h}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t)-\kappa^{h}\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)))\,dx.

(2.37)

From (
2.35
) and (
2.37
) we conclude

\left|\int_{\Omega}\pi_{1/h}(\kappa^{h}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t)))\,dx\right|\leq\frac{C\varepsilon}{h}\left\{\|e_{h}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\right\}.

(2.38)

Next, we remark that using
\boldsymbol{\xi}(0)=0
, we have

\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}=\left\|\int_{0}^{t}e_{h}\left(\partial_{\tau}\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\right)d\tau\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|e_{h}\left(\partial_{\tau}\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}d\tau.

(2.39)

(
2.33
) now follows from (
2.36
), (
2.38
) and (
2.39
) by using Lemma
2.8
, and
\varepsilon\leq 1
.
∎

The following proposition gives us necessary
a priori
estimates.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let us suppose that

this can always be achieved by translation of coordinate system for every

\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}(t)\,dx=0,\quad\textrm{ for every }t\in[0,T].

(2.40)

1.

Proof.

.
From (

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\eta\kappa^{h}|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)|^{2}\,dx+\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\Omega_{s}^{h}}\mathbb{A}^{h}e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)):e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t))\,dx\right)

\displaystyle+\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}2|e_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t))|^{2}\,dx=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}(t)\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)\,dx.

(2.47)

On the other hand, we have

\begin{split}&\left|\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}(\tau)\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(\tau)\,dx\,d\tau\right|\\
&\leq\sum_{\alpha=1,2}\left(\left|\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}F_{\alpha}^{h}(t){u}^{h}_{\alpha}(t)\,dx\right|+\left|\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}F^{h}_{\alpha}(\tau){u}^{h}_{\alpha}(\tau)\,dx\,d\tau\right|\right)+C\|F^{h}_{3}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\partial_{t}u_{3}^{h}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\\
&\leq C\Bigg(\sum_{\alpha=1,2}\left(\|hF^{h}_{\alpha}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\frac{u_{\alpha}^{h}(t)}{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|h\partial_{t}F^{h}_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(0,t;L^{2}(\Omega))}\|\frac{u_{\alpha}^{h}}{h}\|_{L^{2}(0,t;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))}\right)\\
&\hskip 21.52771pt+\|F^{h}_{3}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\partial_{t}u_{3}^{h}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\Bigg).\end{split}

.

\|F^{h}_{3}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\partial_{t}u_{3}^{h}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\frac{1}{\alpha}\|F_{3}^{h}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\alpha\|\partial_{t}u_{3}^{h}(t)\|^{2},

&\leq C\left(\|\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\pi_{h}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(0,t;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))}\|\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(0,t;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))}\right).\end{split}

Remark 2.12
.

It is standard in the derivation of the plate theory in the context of linearized elasticity that one needs to scale differently in-plane and vertical components of the loads to obtain the limit equations. This is connected with the different scaling of in-plane and vertical components of the displacement (in the
a priori
estimate) and the physically observed fact that for the plate it is much easier to bend than to stretch (see also
[
9
,
8
]
).

2.3.2
A priori estimate for pressure

With Proposition
2.11
at hand it is now possible for us to establish the
a priori
estimate for a pressure. We will need the following proposition and corollary.

Proposition 2.13
.

For every
g\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and every
h
, there exists
\boldsymbol{v}^{h}\in H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
, such that (recall (
1.4
))

\operatorname{div}_{h}\boldsymbol{v}^{h}=g\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\|\boldsymbol{v}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{v}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

(2.51)

where
C
is independent of
h
.

Proof.

We follow the idea of
[
7
]
in a different context.
In order to get the control of scaled gradients, we divide
\Omega
in small plates
\{\Omega^{h}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\subset\Omega
of size
h
, such that
\Omega^{h}_{i}\cap\Omega^{h}_{j}=\emptyset
for all
i\neq j
. Here

\Omega^{h}_{k}:=\omega\times\left(-h/2+kh,h/2+kh\right)\cap\Omega,\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}.

(2.52)

Notice that for every
h
only finitely number of
\Omega_{i}^{h}
are non-empty, i.e. for every
h>0
there exists
n(h)
such that
\Omega_{k}^{h}\neq\emptyset
for
|k|\leq n(h)
and
\Omega_{k}^{h}=\emptyset
, for
|k|>n(h)
. Note that for
|k|<n(h)
we have that

\omega\times(-h/2+kh,h/2+kh)\subset\Omega.

We define
g^{h}
on
\Omega
, by defining it in the following way

g^{h}(x_{1}^{h},x_{2}^{h},x_{3}^{h})=g(x_{1}^{h},x_{2}^{h},x_{3}^{h}/h),\quad\textrm{if }(x_{1}^{h},x_{2}^{h},x_{3}^{h})\in\Omega_{0}^{h},

and we extend it by periodicity in
x_{3}
to whole
\Omega
. We find
\boldsymbol{k}^{h}\in H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
which satisfies

\operatorname{div}\boldsymbol{k}^{h}=g^{h},\quad\|\boldsymbol{k}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{k}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C\|g^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

(2.53)

This can be obtained by solving
\Delta\phi^{h}=g^{h}
on
\Omega
with zero boundary condition on
\omega\times\{-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\}
(and periodic on
\partial\omega\times I
) and putting
\boldsymbol{k}^{h}=\nabla\phi^{h}
.
From (
2.53
) it follows that there exists
\Omega_{k}^{h}
, for
|k|<n(h)
, such that for
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{h}=\boldsymbol{k}^{h}|_{\Omega_{k}^{h}}
, it is satisfied

{\rm div}\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{h}=g^{h}|_{\Omega_{k}^{h}},\quad\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{k}^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\nabla\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{k}^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C\|g^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{k}^{h})},

where
C
is independent of
h
(with constant
C
posssibly larger than in (
2.53
)).
\boldsymbol{v}^{h}
can then be defined by translating
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{h}
on
\Omega_{0}^{h}
and rescaling it to whole
\Omega
,
\boldsymbol{v}^{h}(x):=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{h}(\hat{x},hx_{3}+kh)
. Then we have that (
2.55
) is satisfied, since
\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{v}^{h}(x)=\nabla\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{h}(\hat{x},hx_{3}+kh)
. ∎

The following corollary can be proved in the same way as Proposition
2.13
.
Before stating it, we introduce the space

H_{T}^{1}L^{2}:=\left\{\varphi\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)):\varphi(T)=0\right\}.

(2.54)

Corollary 2.14
.

For every
g\in H_{T}^{1}L^{2}
and every
h
, there exists
\boldsymbol{v}^{h}\in H^{1}(0,T;H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))
, such that
\boldsymbol{v}(T)=0
and

\operatorname{div}_{h}\boldsymbol{v}^{h}(t)=g(t),\ \forall t\in[0,T],\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\|\boldsymbol{v}^{h}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))}+\|\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{v}^{h}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))}\leq C\|g\|_{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}},

(2.55)

where
C
is independent of
h
.

Proof.

We define
\Omega_{k}^{h}
,
g^{h}
,
\boldsymbol{k}^{h}
and
\phi^{h}
in the same way as in Proposition
2.13
, for every
t\in[0,T]
. Note that
\Delta\partial_{t}\phi^{h}=\partial_{t}g^{h}
and
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{k}^{h}=\partial_{t}\phi^{h}
.
(
2.53
) becomes

\operatorname{div}\boldsymbol{k}^{h}(t)=g^{h}(t),\ \forall t\in[0,T],\quad\|\boldsymbol{k}^{h}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{k}^{h}\|_{H^{(}0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C\|g^{h}\|_{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}}.

(2.56)

Again it follows that there exists
\Omega_{k}^{h}
, for
|k|<n(h)
, such that for
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{h}=\boldsymbol{k}^{h}|_{\Omega_{k}^{h}}
, it is satisfied

{\rm div}\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{h}(t)=g^{h}(t)|_{\Omega_{k}^{h}},\ \forall t\in[0,T],\ \|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{h}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{k}^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}))}+\|\nabla\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{h}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{k}^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}))}\leq C\|g^{h}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{k}^{h}))}.

The rest of the proof follows the argument of Proposition
2.13
.
∎

Next proposition establishes
a priori
estimate for the pressure.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let conditions of Proposition
2.11
be satisfied.
Let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{h},p^{h})
be a solution of (
2.27
) with the initial condition (
2.28
).
The following is satisfied:

\|\widehat{p}^{h}\|_{(H_{T}^{1}L^{2})^{\prime}}\leq Ch,

(2.57)

where
C
is independent of
h
and
\widehat{p}^{h}
is the extension by zero of
p^{h}
onto the whole
\Omega
.

Proof.

Using Corollary
2.14
we take for
g\in H_{T}^{1}L^{2}
and
h>0
,
\boldsymbol{v}^{h}\in H^{1}(0,T;H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))
that satisfies
\boldsymbol{v}^{h}(T)=0
and

{\rm div}_{h}\,\boldsymbol{v}^{h}(t)=g(t),\forall t\in[0,T],\quad\|\boldsymbol{v}^{h}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))}+\|\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{v}^{h}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))}\leq C\|g\|_{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}}.

By taking
\boldsymbol{v}^{h}
as a test function in (
2.27
) and using Proposition
2.11
we conclude that

{}_{(H_{T}^{1}L^{2})^{\prime}}\big\langle\frac{\widehat{p}^{h}}{h},g\big\rangle_{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq C\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|g\|_{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

where
C>0
is independent of
h
, which finishes the proof.
∎

3
Quasi-static case

In this section, we analyze the case when
\lim_{h\to 0}\eta(h)=0
. In Section
3.1
we provide the compactness result for the sequence of solutions of (
2.27
).
In order to deal with the limit problem, we have to use the rescaled two-scale convergence, which is defined in Appendix
B
. The reason is that, after rescaling on the canonical domain, the characteristic cell has size
\varepsilon
in in-plane direction and
\varepsilon/h
in the vertical direction. Thus one needs to use the test functions that oscillate with different period in in-plane and vertical direction. Auxiliary compactness claims about rescaled two-scale convergence are also given in Appendix
B
. In Section
3.2
we define the effective tensors appearing in the limit problem and prove some of their properties. In Section
3.3
we obtain the limit model, while in Section
3.4
we prove the existence and uniqueness result and energy-type equality for the limit problem.
For the definition of the weak solution, existence and uniqueness result we use the results from
[
21
]
. Here, however, one needs to put an additional effort to define the appropriate operators, since the limit equations do not decouple, see the proof of Theorem
3.16
below. In Section
3.5
we prove the strong convergence of the solutions of (
2.27
) to the solution of the limit problem with appropriate correctors, while in Section
3.6
we discuss the possible generalization of Assumptions
2.2
, as well as the possibility of having surface loads, non-zero initial conditions, non-periodic boundary conditions at the transverse boundary
and the situation when fluid part touches the upper and lower boundary of
\Omega^{h}
. We recall that
\bar{\kappa}=\int_{I}\kappa\,dx_{3}
(see
1.1
) and
\kappa
is the weak star limit of
\kappa^{h}
as
h\to 0
, consequently a function on
\omega
, bounded from below and above by a positive constant.

We additionally introduce the following notation.
We denote by

L_{\kappa,0}:=\left\{(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1},\psi_{2})\in H^{1}_{\#}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{2}_{\#}(\omega):\int_{\Omega}\kappa(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1}-x_{3}\nabla\psi_{2})\,dx=0,\,\int_{\omega}\bar{\kappa}\psi_{2}\,d\hat{x}=0\right\},

and by
M=L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(J_{K})\oplus L^{2}(J_{p}\backslash J_{K}))
.
Note that

L_{1,0}=\dot{H}^{1}_{\#}(\omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})\times\dot{H}^{2}_{\#}(\omega).

For
\boldsymbol{w}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we define
\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})\in L^{2}(Y;{\mathbb{R}}^{3\times 3}_{\operatorname{sym}})
in the following way:

\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g}):=e_{y}(\boldsymbol{w})+\text{sym}\left(0|0|\boldsymbol{g}\right).

(3.1)

The definition naturally extends to
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3})\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
valued functions, for e.g. if
(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;

H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3})\times\mathbb{R}^{3}))
, then taking (
3.1
) pointwise in
(x,t)
, we obtain

\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;L^{2}(Y;{\mathbb{R}}^{3\times 3}_{\operatorname{sym}})))
.

3.1
Compactness result

When dealing with evolution problems, if we want to use the results from elliptic (static) problems, we often do averaging in time. For a given
\nu\in L^{1}(0,T)
and a Hilbert space
X
we define the operator
\cdot^{\nu}:L^{\infty}(0,T;X)\to L^{\infty}(X)
in the following way

\boldsymbol{u}^{\nu}:=\int_{0}^{T}\boldsymbol{u}(t)\nu(t)\,dt.

Furthermore, we define:

H_{T}^{1}L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}):=\{\varphi\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y})):\varphi(T)=0\}.

(3.2)

The following theorem gives us the compactness result.
Appropriate definitions of two-scale convergences are given in Section
B
. Recall also the definition of the spaces
\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}_{f/s}^{x_{3}}
given in (
2.14
).

Proof.

is zero outside
