. (
3.12
) follows from (
3.11
), (
3.16
) and (
3.31
). To see this, note that (
3.31
) gives the necessary compactness and that the derivative in time can be moved to the test function by integration by parts in time variable and then we can use (
3.11
) and (
3.16
).
This also gives that
e_{y}(\boldsymbol{u}_{f}^{0})\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}))
. To conclude that
\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{f}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{f};\mathbb{R}^{3})))
we use an approximation with convolution (in time) and Korn’s inequality. Consequently, we have (
3.12
).
The property
\operatorname{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{u}_{f}^{0}={\rm tr}\,e_{y}(\boldsymbol{u}_{f}^{0})=0
follows from convergence (
3.11
), (
3.16
) and the property
\frac{\varepsilon}{h^{2}}\operatorname{div}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}=0
on
\Omega^{h}_{f}
. The fact that
\boldsymbol{u}_{f}^{0}(0)=0
follows from (
2.28
), (
3.11
), (
3.12
) and (
3.16
)
after passing to the limit in

\frac{\varepsilon}{h^{2}}e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t))\chi_{\Omega_{f}^{h}}=\frac{\varepsilon}{h^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}e_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h})(\tau))\chi_{\Omega_{f}^{h}}\,d\tau,

and using Korn’s inequality.
This establishes (
3.6
).
Since for every
\nu\in L^{1}(0,T)
we have from (
3.19
) and (
3.33
)

0=\pi_{1/h}\int_{\Omega}\kappa^{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h})^{\nu}=\pi_{1/h}\int_{\Omega}\kappa^{h}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}^{h}+\boldsymbol{u}_{f}^{h})^{\nu}\to\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h\pi_{1/h}C^{h}(\nu)\int_{\Omega}\kappa^{h}\right),

we conclude that
\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h\pi_{1/h}C^{h}(\nu)\right)=0
. From (
3.18
) we conclude (
3.8
).

Next we show (
3.14
).
The approach of
[
12
]
needs to be adapted to this more complex framework. We take
\varphi\in C^{1}_{\#}(\bar{\Omega})
,
\nu\in L^{2}(0,T)
and compute

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}\left(\frac{\partial_{1}u^{h}_{1}}{h}+\frac{\partial_{2}u^{h}_{2}}{h}+\frac{\partial_{3}u^{h}_{3}}{h^{2}}\right)\varphi(x)\nu(t)\,dx\,dt=-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}\text{div}_{h}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{h}}{h}\varphi(x)\nu(t)\,dx\,dt

(3.34)

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}\left(\frac{\partial_{1}\widehat{u}^{h}_{1}}{h}+\frac{\partial_{2}\widehat{u}^{h}_{2}}{h}+\frac{\partial_{3}\widehat{u}^{h}_{3}}{h^{2}}\right)\varphi(x)\nu(t)\,dx\,dt-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}\left(\frac{\partial_{1}u^{h}_{f,1}}{h}+\frac{\partial_{2}u^{h}_{f,2}}{h}+\frac{\partial_{3}u^{h}_{f,3}}{h^{2}}\right)\varphi(x)\nu(t)\,dx\,dt

(3.35)

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}\left(\frac{\partial_{1}\widehat{u}^{h}_{1}}{h}+\frac{\partial_{2}\widehat{u}^{h}_{2}}{h}+\frac{\partial_{3}\widehat{u}^{h}_{3}}{h^{2}}\right)\varphi(x)\nu(t)\,dx\,dt

(3.36)

\displaystyle\quad+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Omega\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\left(\frac{u^{h}_{f,1}}{h}\partial_{1}\varphi(x)+\frac{u^{h}_{f,2}}{h}\partial_{2}\varphi(x)+\frac{u^{h}_{f,3}}{h^{2}}\partial_{3}\varphi(x)\right)\nu(t)\,dx\,dt

(3.37)

\displaystyle\rightarrow-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\text{div}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{a}(\widehat{x},t)\,\varphi(x)\nu(t)\,dx\,dt+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|x_{3}\text{div}_{\widehat{x}}\nabla_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(\widehat{x},t)\,\varphi(x)\nu(t)\,dx\,dt

(3.38)

\displaystyle\quad-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}\text{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{w}(x,y,t)\varphi(x)\nu(t)\,dy\,dx\,dt

(3.39)

\displaystyle\quad-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|g_{3}(x,t)\varphi(x)\nu(t)\,dx\,dt+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}u^{0}_{f,3}(x,y,t)\partial_{3}\varphi(x)\nu(t)\,dy\,dx\,dt.

(3.40)

Note that in concluding (
3.40
) we can always take test functions
\varphi
that belong to
C^{1}_{\#}(\Omega_{p})\cap H^{1}(\Omega_{p})
.
(
3.14
) then follows directly from (
3.40
) by the density argument and the arbitrariness of
\nu
.

The convergence (
3.13
) (on a subsequence) to some
p\in(H_{T}^{1}L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}))^{\prime}
is a direct consequence of Proposition
2.15
.
11
11

11

To obtain the compactness statement in the context of two-scale convergence one can use Riesz representation theorem for the elements of
H_{T}^{1}L^{2}
.
The fact that
p=0
on
\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}_{s}^{x_{3}}
is then the direct consequence of the fact that
\widehat{p}^{h}=0
on
\Omega^{h}_{s}
.
To prove its independence of
y
i.e.

p=p(x,t)\,\,\text{in}\,\,\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{x_{3}}\times(0,T),

(3.41)

we can choose as test function
\boldsymbol{v}^{h}(x,t)=\varepsilon h\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i}\left(\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\xi^{i}(x)\nu(t)
, such that
\nu\in C^{1}([0,T])
,
\nu(T)=0
,
\xi^{i}\in C_{c}^{1}(\Omega)
,
\textrm{supp }\xi^{i}\subset U_{i}
,
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{c}^{\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(Y_{f}^{i};\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
for
i=1,\dots,m

and plug it in (
2.27
).
We obtain as a consequence of Proposition
2.11

0\leftarrow-_{(H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f})^{\prime}}\big\langle\frac{p^{h}}{h}\cdot\frac{\text{div}_{h}\boldsymbol{v}^{h}}{h}\big\rangle_{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}_{f}}=-_{(H_{T}^{1}L^{2})^{\prime}}\big\langle\frac{p^{h}}{h}\chi_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}\cdot\frac{\text{div}_{h}\boldsymbol{v}^{h}}{h}\big\rangle_{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}}.

(3.42)

Consequently

\displaystyle 0=-\lim_{h\to 0}{}_{(H_{T}^{1}L^{2})^{\prime}}\big\langle\frac{p^{h}}{h}\chi_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}\cdot\frac{\text{div}_{h}\boldsymbol{v}^{h}}{h}\big\rangle_{H_{T}^{1}L^{2}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\int_{\Omega}\int_{Y}\chi_{Y_{f}(x_{3})}(y)\cdot p^{\nu}(x,y)\cdot\text{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i}(y)\xi^{i}(x)\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\int_{\Omega}\int_{{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}p^{\nu}(x,y)\cdot\text{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i}(y)\xi^{i}(x)\,dy\,dx=-\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\int_{\Omega}{}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({Y}_{f}(x_{3});{\mathbb{R}}^{3})}\langle\nabla_{y}p^{\nu}(x,y),\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i}(y)\xi^{i}(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{D}({Y}_{f}(x_{3});{\mathbb{R}}^{3})}\,dx.

Here
\nabla_{y}p^{\nu}
is the distributional gradient of
p^{\nu}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(Y_{f}(x);{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
is the space of Schwartz distributions on
{Y}_{f}(x_{3})
, while
\mathcal{D}({Y}_{f}(x_{3});{\mathbb{R}}^{3})\equiv C_{c}^{\infty}({Y}_{f}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})
with appropriate structure. Since the distributional gradient (for a.e.
x\in\Omega
) of of the restriction of
p^{\nu}(x,\cdot)
on
{Y}_{f}(x_{3})
is zero, this proves the independence of pressure on
y
, see for details e.g.,
[
26
]
(notice that, however, its support is
y
-dependent). ∎

Remark 3.2
.

In (
3.40
) we also used the fact that
u_{f,3}^{h}
is zero on
\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\}
which is a consequence of our geometrical assumption (
2.2
). We will discuss in Section
3.6
the cases when (
2.2
) is not satisfied.

3.2
Effective tensors

Before stating the result on limit equations we need to define the effective tensors.

1.

We define the effective fourth order tensor: For
\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{C},\boldsymbol{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\text{sym}}
, we define (recall (
2.13
))

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{C},\boldsymbol{D}):=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad\int_{I}\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3})}\mathbb{A}(x_{3},y)\left[\iota(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B})+\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w}_{1,\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}},\boldsymbol{g}_{1,\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}})\right]:\left[\iota(\boldsymbol{C}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{D})\right]dydx_{3},

(3.43)

where
(\boldsymbol{w}_{1,\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}},\boldsymbol{g}_{1,\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}})\in L^{2}(I;\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3}))\times L^{2}(I;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is the unique solution of

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{I}\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3})}\mathbb{A}(x_{3},y)\left[\iota(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B})+\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w}_{1,\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}},\boldsymbol{g}_{1,\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}})\right]:\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{r})\,dy\,dx_{3}=0;

(3.44)

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in L^{2}(I;H^{1}_{\#}(\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})),\ \boldsymbol{r}\in L^{2}(I;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

2.

For
x_{3}\in J_{p}
,we define the second order tensor

\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}):=\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3})}\mathbb{A}(x_{3},y)\,\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,x_{3}}\right)\,dy,

(3.45)

where
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,x_{3}})\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the unique solution of

\begin{split}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3})}\mathbb{A}(x_{3},y)\,\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,x_{3}}):\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{r})\,dy&=\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}\,\left(\text{div}_{y}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+r_{3}\right)\,dy,\\
&\forall\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3}),\ \boldsymbol{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\end{split}

(3.46)

where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
is any extension of
\zeta
to
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
.
12
12

12

The right hand side of (
3.46
)is actually independent of this extension, which can be seen by using Gauss formula that converts the integral over
\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})
in the integral over
\partial\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})=\partial\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3})
. However we write it in this way because of the way how Theorem
3.7
is proved.

3.

For
x_{3}\in J_{p}
, we define the second order tensor

\mathbb{C}^{H}_{ij}(x_{3}):=-\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}\text{tr}\,\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{ij}_{x_{3}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\boldsymbol{g}^{ij}_{x_{3}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\,dy,

(3.47)

where for
i,j=1,2,3

(\boldsymbol{w}^{ij}_{x_{3}},\boldsymbol{g}^{ij}_{x_{3}})\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the unique solution of the following cell problem:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3})}\mathbb{A}(x_{3},y)\left[\boldsymbol{e}^{i}\odot\boldsymbol{e}^{j}+\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w}_{x_{3}}^{ij},\boldsymbol{g}_{x_{3}}^{ij})\right]:\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{r})\,dy=0,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3}),\ \boldsymbol{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

(3.48)

and
\widehat{w}^{ij}_{x_{3}}
is any extension of

{w}^{ij}_{x_{3}}
to
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})

13
13

13

Again, it can be shown, by using Gauss formula, that the expression (
3.47
) is independent of this extension.
.

4.

For
x_{3}\in J_{p}
, we define the second order tensor

\mathbb{K}_{ij}(x_{3}):=\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}q^{i}_{j,x_{3}}\,dy.\quad\text{(the permeability tensor)},\quad i,j=1,2,3,

(3.49)

where for
i=1,2,3
,
\mathbf{q}^{i}_{x_{3}}\in H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\pi^{i}_{x_{3}}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3}))
(
\equiv L^{2}(Y_{f}(x_{3}))
) are the weak solutions on torus of
14
14

14

Recall that a function belonging to
H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})
, when extended by zero, belongs to
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
.
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\begin{cases}-\Delta_{y}\boldsymbol{q}^{i}_{x_{3}}+\nabla_{y}\pi^{i}_{x_{3}}=\boldsymbol{e}^{i},\quad\text{in}\,\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})&\\
\text{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{q}^{i}_{x_{3}}=0,\quad\text{in }\,\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3}).\end{cases}&\textrm{i.e.}&\begin{cases}\int_{Y_{f}(x_{3})}\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{q}^{i}_{x_{3}}\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,dy-\int_{Y_{f}(x_{3})}\pi_{x_{3}}^{i}\textrm{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,dy=\int_{Y_{f}(x_{3})}\psi_{3}\,dy,&\\
\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3});{\mathbb{R}}^{3});&\\
\operatorname{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{q}_{x_{3}}^{i}=0,\quad\text{in }\,\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3}).\end{cases}\end{array}

(3.50)

5.

For
x_{3}\in J_{p}
, we define scalar

M_{0}(x_{3}):=\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}\text{tr}\,\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}}_{2,x_{3}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,x_{3}})\,dy,

(3.51)

where again
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,x_{3}})\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
are unique solutions of
(
3.46
) and
\widehat{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}}_{2,x_{3}}
is any extension of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}}
to
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
.
15
15

15

Again, by using Gauss formula, the expression (
3.51
) is independent of the extension.

The following proposition gives us the important properties of the tensors defined above

Proposition 3.3
.

The solutions of (
3.44
), (
3.46
), (
3.48
), (
3.50
) exist and are unique. The tensors
\mathbb{B}^{H}
,
\mathbb{C}^{H}
,
\mathbb{K}
,
M_{0}
are piecewise constant and following properties are satisfied:

1.

The tensor
\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}
is symmetric and there exists
\nu_{\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}}
such that

\nu_{\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}}^{-1}(|\boldsymbol{A}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{B}|^{2})\leq\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})\leq\nu_{\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}}(|\boldsymbol{A}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{B}|^{2}),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}_{\rm sym}^{2\times 2}.

2.

For all
x_{3}\in J_{p}
, the tensors
\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})
,
\mathbb{C}^{H}(x_{3})
are symmetric and
\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})=\mathbb{C}^{H}(x_{3})
.

3.

For all
x_{3}\in J_{p}
, the tensor
\mathbb{K}(x_{3})
is symmetric and positive semidefinite and there exists
\nu_{\mathbb{K}}>0
such that

\begin{split}&\nu_{\mathbb{K}}\leq\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\leq\nu_{\mathbb{K}}^{-1},\quad i=1,2,3,\,\forall x_{3}\in J_{K};\\
&\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})=0,\quad\forall x_{3}\in J_{p}\backslash J_{K}.\end{split}

(3.52)

4.

There exists
\nu_{M_{0}}>0
such that
M_{0}(x_{3})\geq\nu_{M_{0}}>0,\,\forall x\in\Omega_{p}.

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of the solutions of (
3.44
), (
3.46
), (
3.48
), (
3.50
) go by standard arguments, using Lax-Milgram and (
2.10
).
To prove the claim 1., notice that

\left(\boldsymbol{w}_{1,\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}}(x_{3},\cdot),\boldsymbol{g}_{1,\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}}(x_{3},\cdot)\right)=\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1,2}[\boldsymbol{A}_{\alpha\beta}\left(\boldsymbol{w}^{\alpha\beta}_{x_{3}},\boldsymbol{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{x_{3}}\right)-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}_{\alpha\beta}\left(\boldsymbol{w}^{\alpha\beta}_{x_{3}},\boldsymbol{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{x_{3}}\right)],

(3.53)

where
\left(\boldsymbol{w}^{\alpha\beta}_{x_{3}},\boldsymbol{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{x_{3}}\right)
are defined in (
3.48
).
This can be seen by taking
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\xi\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
,
\boldsymbol{r}=\xi\widetilde{\boldsymbol{r}}
, where
\xi\in L^{2}(I)
,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3});{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
in (
3.44
), and use linearity of (
3.48
). By using the extension on
\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})
(see
[
24
, Chapter 4]
), for each
\boldsymbol{w}^{\alpha\beta}_{x_{3}}
, for
\alpha,\beta=1,2
, the claim 1. follows the proof of
[
8
, Proposition 3.4]
.
To prove the claim 2., notice that the symmetricity of
\mathbb{C}^{H}
is obvious, since
\boldsymbol{w}^{ij}_{x_{3}}=\boldsymbol{w}^{ji}_{x_{3}}
,
\boldsymbol{g}^{ij}_{x_{3}}=\boldsymbol{g}^{ji}_{x_{3}}
, for
i,j=1,2,3
. We prove
\mathbb{B}^{h}=\mathbb{C}^{H}
.

Using
\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,x_{3}}\right)
as test functions in (
3.48
) and by virtue of (
3.46
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}^{H}_{ij}(x_{3})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3})}\left(\mathbb{A}(x_{3},y)\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,x_{3}}\right)\right)_{ij}\,dy=\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3})}\mathbb{A}(x_{3},y)\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,x_{3}}\right):\left(\boldsymbol{e}^{i}\odot\boldsymbol{e}^{j}\right)\,dy

(3.54)

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3})}\mathbb{A}(x_{3},y)\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,x_{3}}\right):\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}\left(\boldsymbol{w}^{ij}_{x_{3}},\boldsymbol{g}^{ij}_{x_{3}}\right)\,dy=-\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}\text{tr}\,\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{w}}^{ij}_{x_{3}},\boldsymbol{g}^{ij}_{x_{3}})\,dy=\mathbb{C}^{H}_{ij}(x_{3}).

(3.55)

Next, we prove the claim 3.:
By testing (
3.50
) with
\mathbf{q}^{j}_{x_{3}}
, for
j=1,2,3
, we obtain

\mathbb{K}_{ij}(x_{3})=\langle\nabla\mathbf{q}^{i}_{x_{3}},\nabla\mathbf{q}^{j}_{x}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})},\quad i,j=1,2,3.

(
3.52
) follows from the fact that
\mathbf{q}^{3}_{x_{3}}
is constant
only if it is zero. On the other hand
\mathbf{q}^{3}_{x_{3}}=0
is a solution of (
3.50
) for
i=3
if and only if
\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})
doesn’t intersect
\{y_{3}=0\}
(in this case
\pi_{x_{3}}^{i}(y)=y_{3}
).
This can be seen directly from the weak form of (
3.50
), by doing integration by part in the second term on the left hand side.

Uniformity with respect to
x_{3}
is the consequence of the geometric assumptions, where only finite number of different domains
\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})
appear.
It remains to prove 4. By virtue of (
3.46
) we have

M_{0}(x_{3})=\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3})}\mathbb{A}(x_{3},y)\,\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,x_{3}}):\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,x_{3}})\,dy>0.

(3.56)

Again, the uniformity with respect to
x_{3}
, is the consequence of the geometric assumptions.
∎

3.3
Limit equations

Before proving the main theorem which gives the limit problem we prove Lemma
3.5
which is fundamental for constructing the appropriate test functions from which we will be able to conclude the condition that the limit pressure satisfies at the interface.
For proving Lemma
3.5
we need a certain construction of Lipschitz sets that is used in Lemma
3.5
and that is provided in Lemma
3.4
.

For
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
r>0
we denote by
G(p,r)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
the infinite cylinder whose base is
B(p,r)

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
C_{i}\subset Y
,
i=1,2
, be connected, Lipschitz sets that are associated sets of open, Lipschitz sets
\mathcal{C}_{i}\subset\mathcal{Y}
,
i=1,2
, respectively. Assume that
C_{1}\cap C_{2}\cap\{y_{3}=0\}\neq\emptyset
. Then there exist sets
\widetilde{C}_{i}\subset Y
,
i=1,2
, that are associates sets of

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{i}\subset\mathcal{Y}
,
i=1,2
, respectively such that:
satisfy:

(i)

For
i=1,2
,
\widetilde{C}_{i}
are connected, with smooth boundary and
\overline{\widetilde{C}}_{i}\backslash\{y_{3}=1\}\subset C_{i}
;

(ii)

For
i=1,2
,
\overline{\widetilde{C}}_{i}
doesn’t intersect the boundary of
\partial Y
except at
\{y_{3}\in\{0,1\}\}
, i.e.
we have

\overline{\widetilde{C}_{i}}\cap\partial Y=\overline{\widetilde{C}_{i}}\cap\{y_{3}\in\{0,1\}\},\quad i=1,2.

(iii)

There exists
p\in(0,1)^{2}
,
\varepsilon>0
such that

\widetilde{C}_{1}\cap\{y_{3}\in[0,\varepsilon]\cup[1-\varepsilon,1)\}=\widetilde{C}_{2}\cap\{y_{3}\in[0,\varepsilon]\cup[1-\varepsilon,1)\}=G(p,\varepsilon)\cap\{y_{3}\in[0,\varepsilon]\cup[1-\varepsilon,1)\}.

Proof.

We will construct the sets
\widetilde{C}_{i}
as cylindrical sets around certain simple (non-intersecting) smooth curve that is contained in
C_{i}
and that doesn’t intersect
\partial Y
or
\partial C_{i}
(except at endpoints). To this end we take
p\in(0,1)^{2}
that
(p,0)\in C_{1}\cap C_{2}
. As a consequence of the fact that
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
are open, there exists
\widetilde{r}>0
such that
(B((p,0),\widetilde{r})\cup B((p,1),\widetilde{r}))\cap Y\subset C_{1}\cap C_{2}
. Since
C_{i}
is connected, for
i=1,2
, there exists a polygonal line
(p,0)s^{i}_{1}\dots s^{i}_{n}(p,1)
contained in
C_{i}
that doesn’t intersect the boundary of
C_{i}
except at points
(p,0),(p,1)
. Without loss of generality we can assume that

\operatorname{dist}(s^{i}_{j},(p,0))>\widetilde{r},\ \operatorname{dist}(s^{i}_{j},(p,1))>\widetilde{r}\quad j=1,\dots,n.

(3.57)

Since both
C_{i}
are open, by slightly perturbing the points
s^{i}_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,n
, we can assume that there are no four coplanar points in the set
\{(p,0),s^{i}_{1},\dots,s^{i}_{n},(p,1)\}
and that the condition (
3.57
) is satisfied. Since there are no four coplanar points the polygonal line
(p,0)s_{1}^{i}\dots s_{n}^{i}(p,1)
is simple. We add four more points in the line in the following way: we define
t^{i}
as intersection of the line
(p,0)s^{i}_{1}
with
B((p,0),\widetilde{r})
and
q^{i}
as intersection of the line
s^{i}_{n-1}(p,1)
with
B((p,1),\widetilde{r})
. We define the polygonal line
(p,0)(p,\widetilde{r}/2)t^{i}s^{i}_{1}\dots s^{i}_{n}q^{i}(p,1-\widetilde{r}/2)(p,1)
. The new polygonal line is contained in
C_{i}
, doesn’t intersect the boundary of
C_{i}
except at the endpoints and is simple. Then we can smoothen it at its corners in a way that it keeps these properties, i.e. we can, for
i=1,2
, find a smooth curve
c^{i}:[a,b]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
(
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
a<b
) that satisfies

\circ

c^{i}(a)=(p,0)
,
c^{i}(b)=(p,1)
;

\circ

c^{i}
is injective, its image (except at the last point) is contained in
C_{i}
;

\circ

\{c^{i}(t):t\in(a,b)\}\cap\partial Y=\{c^{i}(t):t\in(a,b)\}\cap\partial C_{i}=\emptyset
;

\circ

there exists
\widetilde{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\{c^{i}(t):t\in[a,b]\}\cap\{y_{3}\in[0,\widetilde{\varepsilon}]\cup[1-\widetilde{\varepsilon},1)\}=\{c^{i}(t):t\in[a,a+\widetilde{\varepsilon}]\cup[b-\widetilde{\varepsilon},b]\}=(p,0)(p,\widetilde{\varepsilon})\cup(p,1-\widetilde{\varepsilon})(p,1).

The domain
\widetilde{C}_{i}
we can define as the (small enough) cylindrical neighbourhood of the curve
c^{i}
, using its Frenet frme, This is diffeomorphic to cylinder, i.e. there exists
\widehat{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\overline{\widetilde{C}}_{i}
is diffeomorphic to

\overline{B(0,\widehat{\varepsilon})}\times[a,b]

(see
[
28
]
for a short elementary proof or
[
10
, Theorem 3.1-1]
for the proof in the context of shells).
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

1.

If
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{Y}
is an open Lipschitz set that has empty intersection with
\{y_{3}=0\}
,
then every
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{C};\mathbb{R}^{3})
16
16

16

Again, recall that a function
\boldsymbol{\tau}
, when extended by zero, belongs to
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
.
that satisfies
\operatorname{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\tau}=0
satisfies also
\int_{Y}\tau_{3}\,dy=0
.

2.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\subset\mathcal{Y}
, for
i=1,2
are non-empty, open Lipschitz sets that satisfy and
|\mathcal{Y}\backslash\mathcal{C}_{i}|>0
and
whose associated set
C_{i}\subset Y
is connected. Moreover, we assume that that
\mathcal{C}_{1}\cap\mathcal{C}_{2}\cap\{y_{3}=0\}\neq\emptyset
. Then for
i=1,2
there exists
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{i};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
such that for the extensions by zero of
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i}
on whole
\mathcal{Y}
(still denoted by
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i}
), we have
\operatorname{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{1}=\operatorname{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{2}=0
,
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{1}=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{2}
on the set
\{y_{3}=0\}
and
\int_{Y}\tau^{1}_{3}\,dy=\int_{Y}\tau^{2}_{3}\,dy\neq 0
.

Proof.

To prove the first claim we take
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{C};\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\operatorname{div}\boldsymbol{\tau}=0
and by doing integration by parts we conclude

0=\int_{C}\operatorname{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\tau}\cdot y_{3}\,dy=-\langle\boldsymbol{\tau},\nabla_{y}y_{3}\rangle_{L^{2}(C;\mathbb{R}^{3})}=-\int_{Y}\tau_{3}\,dy,

which concludes the proof.

To prove the second claim we take for
i=1,2
the sets
C_{i}
which are associated sets of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and make the construction from Lemma
3.4
, obtaining the sets
\widetilde{C}_{i}
that satisfy (i)-(iii) of Lemma
3.4
.
We take an arbitrary non-zero smooth non-negative function
g:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with support on
B(p,\varepsilon)
(recall (iii) of Lemma
3.4
) and extend it to
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
by taking it independent of
x_{3}
variable (this extension we still denote by
g
).
By using
[
17
, Lemma 2.2]
we can for
i=1,2
find
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{i}\in H^{1}(\widetilde{C}^{\varepsilon/2}_{i};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
such that

\operatorname{div}_{y}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{i}=0,\quad\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{i}|_{\partial\widetilde{C}^{\varepsilon/2}_{i}\cap\{y_{3}=\varepsilon/2\textrm{ or }y_{3}=1-\varepsilon/2\}}=g\boldsymbol{e}^{3},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{i}|_{\partial\widetilde{C}^{\varepsilon/2}_{i}\backslash\{y_{3}=\varepsilon/2\textrm{ or }y_{3}=1-\varepsilon/2\}}=0.

Here

\widetilde{C}_{i}^{\varepsilon/2}:=\widetilde{C}_{i}\backslash\left(G(p,\varepsilon)\cap\{y_{3}\in[0,\varepsilon/2]\cup[1-\varepsilon/2,1)\}\right),

which is obviously Lipschitz and connected.
Next we extend
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{i}
on whole
\widetilde{C}_{i}
, denoting this extension also by
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{i}
, by taking

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{i}:=g\boldsymbol{e}^{3}\textrm{ on }G(p,\varepsilon)\cap\{y_{3}\in[0,\varepsilon/2]\cup[1-\varepsilon/2,1)\}.

Due to the periodic boundary condition we easily see that these extensions satisfy
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{i}\in H_{0}^{1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{i};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
, for
i=1,2
,
and are also
divergence free. To obtain
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{i};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
, we can use convolution with mollifier supported in a small enough neighbourhood of zero. It is important to note that the condition
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{1}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{2}
on
\{y_{3}=0\}
is preserved with convolution (for mollifier supported in small enough neighbourhood of zero) since
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{1}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{2}
in a neighbourhood of
\{y_{3}=0\}
. To check the last property note that by integration by parts for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{C}_{i};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
we have

0=\int_{C_{i}}\operatorname{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\tau}\cdot y_{3}\,dy=-\langle\boldsymbol{\tau},\nabla_{y}y_{3}\rangle_{L^{2}(C_{i};\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\int_{\partial C_{i}\cap\{y_{3}=1\}}\tau_{3}\,d\hat{y}=-\int_{C_{i}}\tau_{3}\,dy+\int_{\partial C_{i}\cap\{y_{3}=1\}}\tau_{3}\,d\hat{y},

from which it follows, using periodicity, that

\int_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\tau_{3}^{1}\,dy=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\cap\{y_{3}=0\}}\tau^{1}_{3}\,d\hat{y}=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{2}\cap\{y_{3}=0\}}\tau^{2}_{3}\,d\hat{y}=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}\tau_{3}^{2}\,dy.

∎

Remark 3.6
.

If we apply Lemma
3.5
2 in the case
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}_{2}=\mathcal{C}

\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{Y}
is an open set with Lipschitz boundary that has non-empty intersection with

, we have the existence of
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{C};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
that satisfies
\int_{\mathcal{C}}\tau_{3}\,dy\neq 0
.

(3.73)

\xi\in C_{c}^{1}(\omega\times(d_{i+1}-\delta,d_{i+1}+\delta))
for
\delta>0
small enough (recall (
2.5
)). We define (recall (
2.6
) and (
2.7
)):

\boldsymbol{\xi}^{h}(x)=\chi_{(d_{i}^{h},d_{i+1}^{h}]}(x_{3})\xi(x)\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)+\chi_{(d_{i+1}^{h},d_{i+2}^{h}]}(x_{3})\xi(x)\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i+1}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right).

(3.74)

As a consequence of (
3.73
) we have that

\boldsymbol{\xi}^{h}\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
.
Moreover we easily conclude that

\displaystyle\varepsilon\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{h}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{2-r}\chi_{(d_{i},d_{i+1}]}(x_{3})\xi(x)\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i}(y)+\chi_{(d_{i+1},d_{i+2}]}(x_{3})\xi(x)\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i+1}(y),

(3.75)

\displaystyle h\operatorname{div}_{h}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{h}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{2-r}\chi_{(d_{i},d_{i+1}]}(x_{3})\partial_{3}\xi(x)\tau^{i}_{3}(y)+\chi_{(d_{i+1},d_{i+2}]}(x_{3})\partial_{3}\xi(x)\tau^{i+1}_{3}(y)

(3.76)

Next we plug in
(\ref {varformporous2gamma0may10})

the function
h^{2}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{h}(x)\varphi(t)
,
where
\varphi\in C^{1}([0,T])
satisfies
\varphi(T)=0
. As in the case for (
3.71
), using (
3.72
), we conclude that

\begin{split}&2\int_{\omega\times(d_{i},d_{i+1})}\xi\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i}}e_{y}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{f}\right):e_{y}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i})\,dy\,dx\\
&+2\int_{\omega\times(d_{i+1},d_{i+2})}\xi\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i+1}}e_{y}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{f}\right):e_{y}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{i+1})\,dy\,dx\\
&-\int_{\omega\times(d_{i},d_{i+2})}p\partial_{3}\xi\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{1}}\tau^{i}_{3}(y)\,dy\,dx=0.\end{split}

(3.77)

From this we easily conclude that
\partial_{3}p\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega\times(d_{i+1}-\delta,d_{i+1}+\delta)))
. Together with previous conclusion, this implies that
p\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(J_{K}))
.

Note that for given
p\in L^{2}(0,T;M)
, the solution of (
3.71
) that satisfies (
3.6
) is unique, which can be easily checked by subtraction.
If
x_{3}\in I_{i}\subset J_{p}\backslash J_{k}
, for some
i=1,\dots,m
, we easily conclude from
(
3.71
) and Lemma
3.5
1 that
\boldsymbol{u}_{f}^{0}(x,y,t)=0
.
This can be seen by taking in (
3.71
) the test functions of the form

\boldsymbol{\xi}(\hat{x},x_{3},y)=\xi_{1}(\hat{x})\xi_{2}(x_{3})\boldsymbol{\tau}(y)
, where
\xi_{1}\in L^{2}(\omega)
,
\xi_{2}\in H_{0}^{1}(I_{i})
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{i};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
such that
\textrm{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\tau}=0
.

For
x_{3}\in J_{K}
the differential form of (
3.71
) is (for fixed
x\in\Omega
)

-\Delta_{y}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{f}+(0,0,\partial_{3}p)+\nabla_{y}\pi=(0,0,0),

(3.78)

which we rewrite as
21
21

21

Recall that
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{f}^{0}
belongs to
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
, when extended by zero outside
\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})
.

\begin{cases}-\Delta_{y}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{f}+\nabla_{y}\pi=-(0,0,\partial_{3}p),&\\
\text{div}_{y}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{f}=0,\quad\left.\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{f}\right|_{\partial\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}=0.&\end{cases}

(3.79)

We conclude that, for
x_{3}\in J_{K}
,
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{f}
and
\pi
have a representation:

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{f}(x,y,t)=-\boldsymbol{q}_{x_{3}}^{3}(y)\partial_{3}p(x,t),&\\
\displaystyle\pi=-\pi^{3}_{\gamma}(y)\partial_{3}p(x,t),&\end{cases}

(3.80)

where
\boldsymbol{q}^{3}_{x_{3}}
is defined in (
3.50
) and extended by zero outside
\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})
.
Note that the first equation in (
3.80
) is also valid for
x_{3}\in J_{p}\backslash J_{K}
, since
\boldsymbol{q}_{x_{3}}^{3}=0
there (cf. proof of Proposition
3.3
3).

To prove (
3.59
) we use (
3.14
). We take
\varphi\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(J_{p}))
,
\varphi(T)=0
, and use
\partial_{t}\varphi(t)
as a test function in (
3.14
) for every
t\in(0,T)
and integrate over the interval
(0,T)
. We then integrate by parts the previous to the last term on the left hand side of (
3.14
) and use (
3.68
), (
3.80
) and Proposition
3.3
2 & 3 as well as the density argument.

∎

Remark 3.8
.

The first equation in (
3.80
) tells us that in the effective Darcy’s law only the derivative of the pressure in the vertical direction is present (unlike in the bulk model where the full gradient is present). By integrating the first equation in (
3.80
) over
\mathcal{Y}
we obtain that the mean fluid velocity is given by

\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{f}^{0}(\cdot,y)\,dy=-\partial_{3}p\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}}\boldsymbol{q}_{x}^{3}(y)\,dy.

Remark 3.9
.

In order to conclude that pressure belongs to
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(J_{K}))
we needed to argument the continuity of traces over the interface where there is a flow (see Section
2.1
). This was done by using test functions that are supported in the neighbourhood of the interface. Such test functions need to be be weakly differentiable, to have support on the fluid part and to satisfy the condition that the third component has non-zero mean value over the torus. Note that, as a consequence of Lemma
3.5
1, it is not possible that the support of such fastly oscillating test function doesn’t intersect the part of
\mathcal{Y}
given by
\{y_{3}=0\}
. Thus we needed to be be careful to constructing such test functions at the interface, since the changing of the domain of the fluid required that we change them from one region to the other, while keeping the continuity property.

The conclusion that the limit pressure
p
belongs to the space
L^{2}(0,T;M)
has the following consequences (cf. Remark
2.3
):

1.

on the interface between
U_{i}
and
U_{i+1}
that satisfies the property that there is no flow, the trace of the pressure may have jump. However from the space of test functions we conclude that
\partial_{3}p=0
on the interface (Neumann boundary condition);

2.

on the interface between
U_{i}
and
U_{i+1}
that satisfies the property that there is flow, the trace of the pressure is continuous.

Remark 3.10
.

Here we compare our model with the ones obtained in
[
27
,
22
]
. In
[
27
]
the author derives the flexural (bending) plate equations (i.e., the equations for
\mathfrak{b}
), starting from 3D Biot’s equations (using isotropic elasticity and assuming the force term being zero, but with some boundary conditions), under the following assumptions:

1.

Normals to the middle surface of the solid skeleton (
x_{3}=0
) remain straight and normal during deformation (this is equivalent to assuming Kirchoff-Love ansatz, cf. (
3.8
) and see
[
9
]
).

2.

The plate is in a state of approximate plane stress.

3.

In plane fluid-velocity gradients relative to the solid are small compared to the transverse fluid-velocity gradient (this is eqzivalent to assuming that the term
\partial_{3}p
is dominant in
\nabla p
).

The equations obtained in this way are then justified in
[
22
]
from 3D Biot’s (quasi-static) theory (again assuming isotropic elasticity) by using the approach from
[
9
]
. Moreover, in
[
22
]
the membrane equations (for the part of in-plane components
\mathfrak{a}
) are obtained and they are decoupled from the bending equations (the equations for
\mathfrak{b}
).

Note that in the case when
M_{0}
,
|\mathcal{Y}_{f}|
and
\mathbb{B}^{H}
are
x_{3}
independent. one can also separate
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{b}
appearing in (
3.59
) by using test functions that are independent of
x_{3}
and the ones that are
x_{3}
dependent and satisfy
\int_{I}\varphi\,dx_{3}=0
for every
\hat{x}\in\omega
(this is how (
3.59
) is written in
[
22
]
).

It is not unusual that non-decoupling of membrane and bending plate equations doesn’t happen in our case, when there are heterogeneities across thickness. This is also obtained in
[
6
]
and in
[
8
]
in some regimes in a different context.

3.4
Analysis of the limit equations

We will slightly modify the system (
3.58
)-(
3.59
).
For
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{1}(0,T;L_{1,0}^{\prime})
,
\boldsymbol{G}\in L^{2}(0,T;M^{\prime})
and
t_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
we will analyze the following problem: find
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},p)\in L^{2}(0,T;L_{1,0})\times L^{2}(0,T;M)
that satisfy

\begin{split}&\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\theta_{3})d\widehat{x}-\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)p\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}))\right]\,d\widehat{x}\\
&+\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}p\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\theta_{3})\right]\,d\widehat{x}={}_{L_{1,0}^{\prime}}\langle\boldsymbol{F}(t),(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*},{\theta}_{3})\rangle_{L_{1,0}},\\
&\quad\forall\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*},{\theta}_{3}\right)\in L_{1,0},\textrm{for a.e. }t\in(0,T),\end{split}

(3.81)

\begin{split}&\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}M_{0}(x_{3})p\,\partial_{t}\varphi dx\,dt-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))\partial_{t}\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}\,dt\\
&+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}\partial_{t}\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}\right)\,d\widehat{x}\,dt+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}p\,\partial_{3}\varphi dx\,dt\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}{{}_{M^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{G},\varphi\rangle_{M}\,dt+\int_{\Omega_{p}}t_{0}\cdot\varphi(0)\,dx,\quad\forall\varphi\in L^{2}(0,T;M)\cap H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{p}))\textrm{ such that }\varphi(T)=0.\end{split}

(3.82)

Thus the loads
\overline{\langle\boldsymbol{F}\rangle_{Y}}
,
\overline{\langle x_{3}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}}
for
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
of the system (
3.58
)-(
3.59
) are naturally replaced with
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{1}(0,T;L_{1,0}^{\prime})
, and we have additional terms on the right hand side of (
3.82
) when compared with (
3.59
).
We have also replaced
L_{{\kappa},0}
by
L_{1,0}
. This can be done without loss of generality, since the solution in space
L_{\kappa,0}
can be easily obtained from the solution in space
L_{1,0}
by a translation for every
t\in[0,T]
. We will give the abstract framework for this problem.
To this end, we adapt the approach from
[
21
]
.
As we will see, adding the additional term on the right hand side of (
3.59
) will change the initial condition for the pressure.

Let
V
be a separable Hilbert space with dual
V^{\prime}
(which is not identified with
V
here). Assume that
V
is densely and continuously embedded into another Hilbert space
H
, which is identified with its dual:

V\hookrightarrow H\equiv H^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V^{\prime}.

Consequently
H
is densely and continuously embedded into
V^{\prime}
.
We denote the inner product on
H
by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H}
and the norm induced by that scalar product by
\|\cdot\|_{H}
. We also denote by
{}_{V^{\prime}}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{V}
the duality pairing between
V
and
V^{\prime}
and by
\|\cdot\|_{V}
the norm on
V
. Suppose that
\mathcal{B}_{0}\in L(H)
and
\mathcal{A}_{0}\in L(V,V^{\prime})
. Let
u_{0}\in H
and
S\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
be given.
We consider the following Cauchy problem: Find
u\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
such that

\begin{cases}\frac{d}{dt}[\mathcal{B}_{0}u]+\mathcal{A}_{0}u=S\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})\\
[\mathcal{B}_{0}u](0)=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{B}_{0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{0}\in H.\end{cases}

(3.83)

The time derivative in (
3.83
) is taken in the sense of distributions.
We give the following definition of the weak solution of (
3.83
) on the time interval
(0,T)
(see
[
21
]
).

Definition 3.11
.

The function
u\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
such that

-\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathcal{B}_{0}u(t),v^{\prime}(t)\rangle_{H}\,dt+\int_{0}^{T}{{}_{V^{\prime}}}\langle\mathcal{A}_{0}u(t),v(t)\rangle_{V}\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}{{}_{V^{\prime}}}\langle S(t),v(t)\rangle_{V}\,dt+\langle\mathcal{B}_{0}u_{0},v(0)\rangle_{H},

(3.84)

holds for all
v\in\{w\in L^{2}(0,T;V)\cap H^{1}(0,T;H):w(T)=0\}

is called a weak solution to (
3.83
).

Remark 3.12
.

Notice that the solution of (
3.83
) satisfies
\mathcal{B}_{0}u\in H^{1}(0,T;V^{\prime})
and thus we can give a meaning to the initial value in (
3.83
).

The following assumption will be needed for the existence result.

Assumption 3.13
.

We assume that

1.

\mathcal{A}_{0}\textrm{ is monotone on }V
, i.e.

{}_{V^{\prime}}\langle\mathcal{A}_{0}v,v\rangle_{V}\geq 0,\quad\forall v\in V;

2.

\mathcal{B}_{0}
is self-adjoint positive semidefinite on
H
;

3.

There exist constants
\lambda,c>0
such that

_{V^{\prime}}\langle\mathcal{A}_{0}v,v\rangle_{V}+\lambda\langle\mathcal{B}_{0}v,v\rangle_{H}\geq c\|v\|_{V}^{2},\quad\forall v\in V.

(3.85)

We have the following theorem as a consequence of
[
21
, Theorem 2.1, Theorem 2.2]
. We will use this theorem to prove the existence and uniqueness result.

Theorem 3.14
.

Under the Assumption
3.13
, there exists a unique solution of (
3.83
) in the sense of Definition
3.11
. The solution satisfies the following stability estimate:

\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}\leq C(c,\lambda)\left[\|S\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+\langle\mathcal{B}_{0}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle_{H}\right],

(3.86)

where
C(c,\lambda)>0
depends only on
c
and
\lambda
.

Remark 3.15
.

If, in addition, we have that
\mathcal{B}_{0}
is coercive on
H
, then it follows from
[
14
, Remark 1, Chapter XVIII.5]
that the solution of (
3.84
) is in
C([0,T];H)
and there exists
C(c,\lambda)>0
such that

\|u\|^{2}_{C(0,T;H)}\leq C(c,\lambda)\left[\|S\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+\langle\mathcal{B}_{0}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle_{H}\right].

(3.87)

Next we state and prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.16
.

For
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{1}(0,T;L_{1,0}^{\prime})
,
\boldsymbol{G}\in L^{2}(0,T;M^{\prime})
there exist a unique solution
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},p)\in L^{2}(0,T;L_{1,0})\times L^{2}(0,T;M)
of (
3.81
)- (
3.82
).

Proof.

We will put our problem in the framework of Theorem
3.14
. For
\widehat{p}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}\in L_{1,0}^{\prime}
we introduce
(\mathfrak{a}^{\widehat{p}}_{0,1},\mathfrak{b}^{\widehat{p}}_{0,1})\in L_{1,0}
and
(\mathfrak{a}_{0,2}^{\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}},\mathfrak{b}_{0,2}^{\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}})\in L_{1,0}
respectively that satisfy:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{0,1}^{\widehat{p}}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,1}^{\widehat{p}}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\theta_{3})d\widehat{x}=\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)\widehat{p}\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}))\right]\,d\widehat{x}

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}\widehat{p}\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\theta_{3})\right]\,d\widehat{x},\quad\forall\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*},{\theta}_{3}\right)\in L_{1,0};

(3.88)

\begin{split}&\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{0,2}^{\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,2}^{\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\theta_{3})d\widehat{x}=_{L_{1,0}^{\prime}}\langle\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}},(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*},{\theta}_{3})\rangle_{L_{1,0}},\quad\forall\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*},{\theta}_{3}\right)\in L_{1,0}.\end{split}

(3.89)

Assuming
\widehat{p}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}
are known, the solutions of (
3.88
)-(
3.89
) are unique as a consequence of Proposition
3.3
and Lax-Milgram.
By assuming
p
known we decompose the solution of (
3.81
) as
(\mathfrak{a}^{p}_{0,1},\mathfrak{b}^{p}_{0,1})+(\mathfrak{a}^{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0,2},\mathfrak{b}^{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0,2})
.
By plugging this in(
3.82
), the solution of (
3.82
) we write in the following way:

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})p\,\partial_{t}\varphi dx\,dt-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))\partial_{t}\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{0,1}^{p}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}\,dt

(3.90)

\displaystyle+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}\partial_{t}\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,1}^{p}\right)\,d\widehat{x}\,dt+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}p\,\partial_{3}\varphi dx\,dt

(3.91)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))\partial_{t}\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{0,2}^{\boldsymbol{F}}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}\,dt

(3.92)

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}\partial_{t}\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,2}^{\boldsymbol{F}}\right)\,d\widehat{x}\,dt+\int_{0}^{T}{{}_{M^{\prime}}}\langle G,\varphi\rangle_{M}\,dt

(3.93)

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega_{p}}t_{0}\cdot\varphi(0)\,dx,\quad\forall\varphi\in L^{2}(0,T;M)\cap H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{p}))\textrm{ such that }\varphi(T)=0.

(3.94)

We put (
3.94
) in the framework of weak solution of (
3.83
), after integration by parts on the right hand side. We now put
V:=M
,
H:=L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
. The operator
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is defined through bilinear form

_{V^{\prime}}\langle\mathcal{A}_{0}v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{V}:=\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}v_{1}(x)\cdot\partial_{3}v_{2}(x)\,dx,\quad\forall v_{1},v_{2}\in V.

(3.95)

Obviously 1 of Assumption
3.13
is satisfied.
The operator
\mathcal{B}_{0}
is also defined through bilinear form

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{B}_{0}h_{1},h_{2}\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})h_{1}(x)\cdot h_{2}(x)\,dx+\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))h_{2}\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{0,1}^{h_{1}}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}

(3.96)

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}h_{2}\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,1}^{h_{1}}\right)\,d\widehat{x},

(3.97)

where
(\mathfrak{a}_{0,1}^{h_{1}},\mathfrak{b}_{0,1}^{h_{1}})\in L_{1,0}
are defined as the solutions of (
3.88
) once
\widehat{p}
is replaced by
h_{1}
.
From (
3.88
) it follows:

\begin{split}&\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))h_{2}dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{0,1}^{h_{1}}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}-\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}h_{2}dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,1}^{h_{1}}\right)\,d\widehat{x}\\
&=\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{0,1}^{h_{1}}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,1}^{h_{1}}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{0,1}^{h_{2}}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,1}^{h_{2}})d\widehat{x}\\
&=\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))h_{1}dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{0,1}^{h_{2}}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}-\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}h_{1}dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,1}^{h_{2}}\right)\,d\widehat{x}.\end{split}

Using this and 4 of Proposition
3.3
we have the positive definitness of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and thus Assumption
3.13
2 is satisfied.
Also, it is easy to see that Assumption
3.13
3 is satisfied.
The operator
S(t)\in V^{\prime}
is defined for
t\in[0,T]
in the following way

{}_{V^{\prime}}\langle S(t),v\rangle_{V}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))v\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{0,2}^{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}(t)}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}

\displaystyle+\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}v\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,2}^{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}(t)}\right)\,d\widehat{x}+_{V^{\prime}}\langle\boldsymbol{G}(t),v\rangle_{V},\ \forall v\in V,

where for
t\in[0,T]
,
(\mathfrak{a}_{0,2}^{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}(t)},\mathfrak{b}_{0,2}^{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}(t)})\in L_{1,0}
are defined as solutions of (
3.89
) once
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}
is replaced by
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}(t)
.
The pressure

p_{0}
is defined through the following relation:

\begin{split}&\langle\mathcal{B}_{0}p_{0},h\rangle_{H}=\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))h\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{0,2}^{\boldsymbol{F}(0)}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}\\
&-\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}h\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,2}^{\boldsymbol{F}(0)}\right)\,d\widehat{x}+\int_{\Omega_{p}}t_{0}\cdot h\,dx\\
&=\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{0,1}^{h}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,1}^{h}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{0,2}^{\boldsymbol{F}(0)}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{0,2}^{\boldsymbol{F}(0)})d\widehat{x}+\int_{\Omega_{p}}t_{0}\cdot h\,dx,\quad\forall h\in H,\end{split}

(3.98)

where
(\mathfrak{a}^{\boldsymbol{F}(0)},\mathfrak{b}^{\boldsymbol{F}(0)})\in L_{1,0}
is defined by (
3.89
). Since
\mathcal{B}_{0}
is a positive definite bounded operator this defines unique
p_{0}\in H
by Riesz representation theorem. We apply Theorem
3.14
to obtain the unique
p\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
. The uniqueness of
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b})\in L^{2}(0,T;L_{1,0})
follows from (
3.58
) and Lax-Milgram.
∎

From (
3.98
) we conclude that
the initial condition
p_{0}
of the system (
3.58
)-(
3.59
) is related with the initial condition
\widetilde{p}_{0}
of the system (
3.81
)-(
3.82
), after simple substitution of the loads, in the following way

\widetilde{p}_{0}=p_{0}+\mathcal{B}^{-1}_{0}t_{0},

(3.99)

where
\mathcal{B}_{0}
is defined by
(
3.97
)
.
The following proposition gives us additional regularity of the solution.

Proposition 3.17
.

If
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{1}(0,T;L_{1,0}^{\prime})
,
\boldsymbol{G}\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
,
t_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, then the solution of (
3.81
)- (
3.82
) satisfies
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},p)\in C([0,T];L_{1,0})\times C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))
.

Proof.

The fact that
p\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))
follows from (
3.94
) and Remark
3.15
. The regularity of
\mathfrak{a}
,
\mathfrak{b}
follows then from (
3.81
).
∎

Remark 3.18
.

The previous proposition fills the gap between the result of Theorem
3.7
and Theorem
3.16
. Namely as a result of Theorem
3.7
we have that the obtained limit is in
L^{\infty}
w.r.t. time, while Theorem
3.16
guarantees only the existence of solution, which is in
L^{2}
w.r.t. time.

The rest of the section is devoted to proving Proposition
3.20
, which is crucial for proving the convergence results in Section
3.5
. Proposition
3.19
will help us to prove Proposition
3.20
.
It gives us additional regularity of solution under the additional regularity of the loads. Proposition
3.20
is then proved by using this regularity and approximation argument. Before stating proving Proposition
3.19
, we define the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
as a self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
defined through the bilinear form:

\widetilde{a}_{0}(v_{1},v_{2}):=\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}v_{1}(x)\cdot\partial_{3}v_{2}(x)\,dx,\quad\forall v_{1},v_{2}\in V.

(3.100)

Note that
\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0})\subset V
and the operator
\mathcal{A}_{0}
defined by (
3.95
) coincides with the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
on
\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0})
.

Proposition 3.19
.

If
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{2}(0,T;L_{1,0}^{\prime})
,
\boldsymbol{G}\in H^{1}(0,T;V^{\prime})
and
\widetilde{p}_{0}\in\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0})
, where
\widetilde{p}_{0}
is defined by (
3.99
) and
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
is defined through bilinear form (
3.100
), then the solution of (
3.81
)- (
3.82
) satisfies
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},p)\in H^{1}(0,T;L_{1,0})\times H^{1}(0,T;M)
.

Proof.

It is easy to see, by taking the derivative in time in (
3.83
), that if in the equation (
3.83
) one takes
S
such that
\frac{d}{dt}S\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
,
u_{0}\in V
such that
\mathcal{A}_{0}u_{0}\in H
, then as a consequence we get that
\frac{d}{dt}u\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
.
If the conditions of the proposition are satisfied, we are exactly in this situation. This can be seen by writing the system (
3.81
) and (
3.82
) in the form of (
3.83
) (cf. (
3.94
)).
From this we conclude the regularity w.r.t. time for
p
. To conclude the regularity w.r.t. time for
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{b}
we use (
3.81
).
∎

Finally we prove the last result of this section. It is the energy-type equality valid for the solution of (
3.81
)-(
3.82
).As usual, such identities are needed to prove the strong convergence result.

Proposition 3.20
.

If
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{1}(0,T;L_{1,0}^{\prime})

\boldsymbol{G}\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
,
t_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, then the solution of (
3.81
)-(
3.82
) satisfies the following identity for every
t\in(0,T)
:

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}(t)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(t))):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}(t)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(t))d\widehat{x}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})p^{2}(t)dx\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})|\partial_{3}p|^{2}dx\,dt={}_{L_{1,0}^{\prime}}\langle\boldsymbol{F}(t),(\mathfrak{a}(t),\mathfrak{b}(t))\rangle_{L_{1,0}}-{}_{L_{1,0}^{\prime}}\langle\boldsymbol{F}(0),(\mathfrak{a}(0),\mathfrak{b}(0))\rangle_{L_{1,0}}\\
&-\int_{0}^{t}{{}_{L_{1,0}^{\prime}}}\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}(\tau),(\mathfrak{a}(\tau),\mathfrak{b}(\tau))\rangle_{L_{1,0}}\,d\tau+\int_{0}^{t}{{}_{V^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{G},p\rangle_{V}\,dt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})(\widetilde{p}_{0})^{2}\,dx,\end{split}

(3.101)

where
\widetilde{p}_{0}
is defined with (
3.99
) (and
p_{0}
with (
3.98
)).

Proof.

The proof goes directly, if we have additional regularity of the solution.
First we consider the system (
3.81
)-(
3.82
) and assume that
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{2}(0,T;L_{1,0}^{\prime})
,
\widetilde{p}_{0}\in\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{A}_{9}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
. Using Proposition
3.19
we conclude that the solution of (
3.81
) and (
3.82
) satisfies
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},p)\in H^{1}(0,T;L_{1,0})\times H^{1}(0,T;V)
.
By doing integration by parts and utilizing a density argument we conclude from (
3.82
) that
p(0)=\widetilde{p}_{0}
and for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
we have

\begin{split}&\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})\partial_{t}p\,\varphi dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}p\,\partial_{3}\varphi dx+\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{\partial_{t}a}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}\\
&-\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}\right)\,d\widehat{x}={{}_{V^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{G},p\rangle_{V}\,dt\quad\forall\varphi\in V.\end{split}

(3.102)

We take
t\in[0,T]
.
By testing (
3.81
) with
(\partial_{\tau}\mathfrak{a},\partial_{\tau}\mathfrak{b})
, integrating over
[0,t]
and adding (
3.102
) tested with

\varphi=p
and then integrated over
(0,t)

we obtain

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}(t)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(t)):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}(t)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(t))d\widehat{x}dt+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})p^{2}(t)dxdt\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}|\partial_{3}p|^{2}dx\,d\tau=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}{{}_{L_{1,0}^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{F},(\partial_{t}\mathfrak{a},\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b})\rangle_{L_{1,0}}\,d\tau+\int_{0}^{T}{{}_{V^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{G},p\rangle_{V}\,dt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})(\widetilde{p}_{0})^{2}\,dx.\end{split}

(3.103)

We obtain (
3.101
) for these more regular loads (and initial condition) by doing integration by parts in the first term on the right hand side of (
3.103
). The statement of the proposition then follows by approximation of the loads and initial condition from the stability results (
3.86
) and (
3.87
) and the stability estimate for the equation (
3.81
), using the fact that
H^{2}(0,T;L_{1,0}^{\prime})
is dense in
H^{1}(0,T;L_{1,0}^{\prime})
and that
\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0})
is dense in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
∎

Remark 3.21
.

By using the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
and under the assumption that
\mathcal{B}_{0}
is coercive, we can use the semigroup theory to obtain the existence for the problem (
3.83
), cf. Section
4.2
below. This, however, gives only the existence in
C([0,T];H)
and in order to conclude the existence in
L^{2}(0,T;V)
as well as the stability estimate (
3.86
) we would have to do approximation of the solution by the Galerkin method as is done in Section
4.2
. Note, however, that putting (
3.83
) in the framework (
4.8
) would be simpler then in the case of non-zero inertial term, since the corresponding operator
\mathcal{A}
would then be positive definite (as usual, we first need to do the transformation of the solution
\boldsymbol{u}(t)\mapsto e^{-\lambda t}u(t)
which converts the condition (
3.85
) in the positive definiteness of the corresponding operator). By using semigroup approach the claim of Proposition
3.19
becomes clear: if the initial condition is regular and the loads are regular the solution of the problem (
4.8
) is, in fact, strong.

In Section
4.2
for the case of non-zero inertial term we used both approaches: semigroup approach and Galerkin approximation.
The semigroup approach doesn’t give us
a
priori enough regularity to prove the existence of the solution, but it gives us a good way to approximate the initial condition and loads to obtain regular enough solution. This is used to prove energy-type equality. The existence of the solution is proved via Galerkin approximation.

3.5
Strong convergence

The strong convergence is proved under the condition
\boldsymbol{F}(0)=0
.
This condition was not needed to derive the limit equations and establish weak convergence (as it was assumed in e.g.
[
23
]
), but for establishing the strong convergence
this seems to be reasonable condition, since under that condition we have that
\mathfrak{a}(0)=0
,
\mathfrak{b}(0)=0
, i.e. the initial conditions are preserved.
We will firstly prove the convergence of energies.

Theorem 3.22
.

Let assumptions (
2.40
) and (
2.42
) be satisfied.
We also suppose that Assumptions
2.2
is satisfied and
\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}\xrightarrow{t,2-r,2}\boldsymbol{F},

\pi_{h}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}\xrightarrow{t,2-r,2}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}
,
where
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
and

\boldsymbol{F}(0)=0
. We have the following convergences:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{s}^{h}}\mathbb{A}^{h}(x)e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)):e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t))\,dx

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}(t)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(t)):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}(t)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(t))d\widehat{x}\,dt

\displaystyle+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}M_{0}(x_{3})p^{2}(t)dx\,dt,

(3.104)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}|e_{h}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\right)|^{2}dx\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{x_{3}}}\left|e_{y}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{f}_{0}\right)\right|^{2}dx\,dy\,dt,

(3.105)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\eta\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,dt

\displaystyle=0.

(3.106)

Here
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},p)\in L^{2}(0,T;L_{\kappa,0})\times L^{2}(0,T;M)
are the solutions of (
3.58
)-(
3.59
) and the function
\boldsymbol{u}^{f}_{0}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})))
is defined with (
3.80
). Furthermore,
(\boldsymbol{u}^{h},p^{h})
is the solution of (
2.27
) with initial condition (
2.28
).

Proof.

We will prove the convergences (
3.104
) and (
3.106
) and instead of (
3.105
) we will firstly prove

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}|e_{h}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\right)|^{2}dx\,d\tau\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{x}}\left|e_{y}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{f}_{0}\right)\right|^{2}dx\,dy\,d\tau\,dt.

(3.107)

By using Remark
2.7
we take
\boldsymbol{v}=\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}
as test function in (
2.31
). This yields for every
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\eta\kappa^{h}|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)|^{2}\,dx+\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\Omega_{s}^{h}}\mathbb{A}^{h}(x)e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)):e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t))\,dx\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}2|e_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t))|^{2}\,dx=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\,dx.

(3.108)

Fixing
t\in[0,T]
, integrating over interval
[0,t]
and then over interval
[0,T]
we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\eta\kappa^{h}|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt+\frac{1}{2h^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{s}^{h}}\mathbb{A}^{h}(x)e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)):e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t))\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle+\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}|e_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(\tau))|^{2}\,dx\,d\tau\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}(\tau)\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(\tau)\,dx\,d\tau\,dt.

(3.109)

Notice that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}(\tau)\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(\tau)\,dx\,d\tau\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}(t)\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)\,dx\,dt-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}(\tau)\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(\tau)\,dx\,d\tau\,dt

(3.110)

\displaystyle\to\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\overline{\langle\boldsymbol{F}\rangle_{Y}}\cdot(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b})\,d\widehat{x}\,dt+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\omega}\overline{\langle x_{3}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(\tau)\rangle_{Y}}\cdot\nabla_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(\tau)\,d\widehat{x}\,d\tau\,dt

(3.111)

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\overline{\langle x_{3}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}\rangle_{Y}}\cdot\nabla_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}\,d\widehat{x}\,dt-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\omega}\overline{\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}(\tau)\rangle_{Y}}\cdot(\mathfrak{a}(\tau),\mathfrak{b}(\tau))\,d\widehat{x}\,d\tau\,dt.

(3.112)

Notice also that

\int_{\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{(}x_{3})}\left|e_{y}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{f}(x,y)\right)\right|^{2}dx\,dy=\int_{\Omega}K_{33}(x)|\partial_{3}p(x,\tau)|^{2}dx,

(3.113)

where we used (
3.50
) and (
3.80
) and the fact that

\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})=\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}q^{3}_{3,x_{3}}(y)\,dy=\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{q}_{x_{3}}^{3},\nabla\boldsymbol{q}_{x_{3}}^{3}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})}=\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}e_{y}(\boldsymbol{q}_{x_{3}}^{3}):e_{y}(\boldsymbol{q}_{x_{3}}^{3})\,dy.

(3.114)

To conclude the last equality we used divergence free condition of
\boldsymbol{q}^{3}_{x_{3}}
.

Using (
3.101
) integrated over
[0,T]
(with
\mathfrak{a}(0)=0
,
\mathfrak{b}(0)=0
,
\widetilde{p}_{0}=0
), from (
3.112
) and (
3.114
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\eta\kappa^{h}|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt+\frac{1}{2h^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{s}^{h}}\mathbb{A}^{h}(x)e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)):e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t))\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle+\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}|e_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(\tau))|^{2}\,dx\,d\tau\,dt\to\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b})d\widehat{x}dt

\displaystyle+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}M_{0}(x_{3})p^{2}dx\,dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{K}_{33}|\partial_{3}p|^{2}dx\,d\tau\,dt.

(3.115)

Note that as a consequence of (
3.43
), (
3.48
), (
3.53
), (
3.56
) and (
3.68
)
we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}_{s}^{x_{3}}}\mathbb{A}(x_{3},y)\left(\iota(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a})-x_{3}\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b})+\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})\right):\left(\iota(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a})-x_{3}\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b})+\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})\right)\,dx\,dy\,dt

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b})d\widehat{x}\,dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}M_{0}(x_{3})p^{2}dx\,dt.

(3.116)

By using lower semicontinuity of the convex energy we have the following inequalities (using (
2.12
), (
3.10
), (
3.12
), (
3.113
) and (
3.116
)):

\begin{split}&\liminf_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{s}^{h}}\mathbb{A}^{h}(x)e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)):e_{h}(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t))\,dx\,dt\geq\\
&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b})d\widehat{x}dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}M_{0}(x_{3})p^{2}dx\,dt,\end{split}

(3.117)

\liminf_{h\to 0}\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}|e_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(\tau))|^{2}\,dx\,d\tau\,dt\geq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}K_{33}(x)|\partial_{3}p(x,\tau)^{2}dx\,d\tau\,dt,

(3.118)

\liminf_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\eta\kappa^{h}|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt\geq\min\{\kappa^{h}\}\eta\liminf_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt\geq 0.

(3.119)

By combining (
3.117
)-(
3.119
) with (
3.115
) we obtain (
3.104
), (
3.106
) and (
3.107
). It remains to prove (
3.105
).
We take a sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{f,n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset H^{1}(0,T;C^{1}(\Omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})))
, such that

\varepsilon\chi_{\Omega_{f}^{h}}(x)e_{x}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{f,n}(x,\frac{x}{\varepsilon})\right)\xrightarrow{t,2-r,2}e_{y}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{f}^{0}).

Notice that (
3.107
) is equivalent with (taking into account (
3.12
)):

\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{f}^{h}}\left|\frac{\varepsilon}{h^{2}}e_{h}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h})-\varepsilon e_{x}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{f,n}(x,\frac{x}{\varepsilon})\right)\right|^{2}dx\,d\tau\,dt=0.

(3.120)

(
3.120
) implies that

\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{h^{4}}\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}|e_{h}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\right)|^{2}dx\,dt=\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\int_{\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})}\left|e_{y}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{f}_{0}\right)\right|^{2}dx\,dy\,dt,\quad\forall T^{\prime}<T.

In order to conclude it also for
T^{\prime}=T
one can extend the forces on the interval
[0,T^{\prime\prime}]
, where
T^{\prime\prime}>T
with constant forces
\boldsymbol{F}^{h}(T)
and then use
the uniqueness of the solution and the above result.
∎

We have the following strong convergence result which includes correctors.

Theorem 3.23
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
3.22
the following convergences hold:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{f}}\left|\frac{\varepsilon}{h^{2}}e_{h}(\partial_{\tau}\boldsymbol{u}^{h})(x,\tau)+e_{y}(\boldsymbol{q}^{3}_{n})\left(x,\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\partial_{3}p(x,\tau)\right|^{2}\,dx\,d\tau=0,

(3.121)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{h}_{s}}\left|\frac{1}{h}e_{h}\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{h}\right)-e_{h}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{a}_{1}(\widehat{x},t)-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}(\widehat{x},t)\\
\mathfrak{a}_{2}(\widehat{x},t)-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}(\widehat{x},t)\\
h^{-1}\mathfrak{b}(\widehat{x},t)\end{pmatrix}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\quad-e_{h}\left(\varepsilon\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{n}(\widehat{x},t))\right)_{\alpha\beta}\boldsymbol{w}^{\alpha\beta}_{n}\left(x,\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+e_{h}\left(\varepsilon x_{3}\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\partial_{\alpha\beta}\mathfrak{b}_{n}(\widehat{x},t)\boldsymbol{w}^{\alpha\beta}_{n}\left(x,\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\right)-e_{h}\left(\varepsilon p_{n}(x,t)\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,n}\left(x,\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad-e_{h}\left(h\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{n}(\widehat{x},t))\right)_{\alpha\beta}\int_{0}^{x_{3}}\boldsymbol{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{n}(x)\,dx_{3}\right)

(3.122)

\displaystyle\left.\quad+e_{h}\left(hx_{3}\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\partial_{\alpha\beta}\mathfrak{b}_{n}(\widehat{x},t)\int_{0}^{x_{3}}\boldsymbol{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{n}(x)\,dx_{3}\right)-e_{h}\left(hp_{n}(x,t)\int_{0}^{x_{3}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,n}(x)\,dx_{3}\right)\right|^{2}dx\,dt=0.

Here
\boldsymbol{q}^{3}_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;C^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3}))

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{q}_{n}^{3}-\boldsymbol{q}^{3}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3}))}\to 0,\quad\|\boldsymbol{q}_{n}^{3}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3}))}\textrm{ bounded};

\displaystyle\|p_{n}-p\|_{L^{2}(0,T;M)}\to 0,\quad\min\{\frac{\varepsilon}{h},h\}\|p_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;C^{1}(\omega))}\to 0;

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,n}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3}))}\to 0,\quad\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{s}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3}))}\textrm{ bounded };

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{g}_{n}^{\alpha\beta}-\boldsymbol{g}^{\alpha\beta}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\to 0,\quad\|\boldsymbol{g}_{n}^{\alpha\beta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\textrm{ bounded};

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,n}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\to 0,\quad\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\textrm{ bounded}.

Here for
x=(\hat{x},x_{3})
we put
\boldsymbol{q}^{3}(x,\cdot):=\boldsymbol{q}^{3}_{x_{3}}(\cdot)
,
\boldsymbol{w}^{\alpha\beta}(x,\cdot):=\boldsymbol{w}^{\alpha\beta}_{x_{3}}(\cdot)
,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2}(x,\cdot):=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2,x_{3}}(\cdot)

), (

Proof.

Using the convexity of the quadratic energy, (
3.121
) is equivalent with
(
3.105
) as a consequence of (
3.80
).
In the similar way (
3.122
) is equivalent with (
3.104
), using (

), (
3.44
) and (
3.68
). To compare, see the proof of
[
