, Proposition 6.1, Theorem 6.1]
.
Additional regularization through the approximation sequences is done in order to make the convergences valid.

∎

3.6
Further discussions on the limit model

In this section we discuss the possible generalization of the Assumptions
2.2
, the possibility of different initial conditions and boundary conditions at the transverse boundary, possibility of imposing surface loads as well as the situation when condition (
2.2
) is not satisfied. Everything discussed here can also be concluded
for the situation with inertial term analyzed in Section
4
.

3.6.1
Different initial and boundary conditions at transverse boundary

It is not a problem to assume different initial conditions (the initial position and initial velocity different than zero). However, in that case one needs to assume certain natural compatibility conditions, see
[
15
, Section 2.2, Section 3.1]
.

Also, non-periodic boundary conditions at the transverse boundary can be imposed (like e.g., Dirichlet boundary conditions, see
[
13
]
for discussion of different possibilities for boundary conditions for Stokes equation). All the estimates obtained in this paper can be easily adapted to Dirichlet boundary conditions (see, e.g.,
[
6
,
8
]
).
However, one would need to do further discussion like analyze separately
C^{1,1}
domain when using results for corrector from Appendix
A
. Periodic boundary conditions simplify this and are also assumed in
[
12
,
23
]
.

3.6.2
Generalization of Assumptions
2.2

By analyzing the proof of Theorem
3.1
and Theorem
3.7
(see also Remark
3.9
) we can see that it was not necessary to demand that the sets
U_{i}
are of the form
U_{i}=\omega\times I_{i}
. Instead, they can be assumed to be generally Lipschitz. It is not difficult to adapt the property of the existence or non-existence of flow, which has to be localized for every point of the interface.
This would imply that the sets
J_{p}
and
J_{K}
defined in (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) depend also on
\hat{x}
. We also emphasize the fact that the contact of elastic and poroelastic plate as well as poroelastic and poroelastic plate that have interface on the plane parallel to
x_{3}
axis doesn’t produce any problems for the analysis (the reason being that only the third derivative of the pressure appears in the limit model).

The other possible generalization might go in the direction of having infinite number of possible cell-types. This is plausible, provided that we can construct the extension operator with controllable constant (see Lemma
2.8
and Lemma
2.9
). As a consequence it is possible to obtain the limit tensors which are not piecewise constants. It is also conceivable that with suitable interface conditions we would obtain the similar limit equations as we obtained here.

3.6.3
Surface loads

From the application point of view it is important to have surface loads on the upper and lower part of the boundary,
\omega\times\{-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\}
. The discussion here resembles
[
8
, Corollary 3.42]
, where the case of elastic high-contrast inclusions is analyzed.

We assume that in (
2.27
), on the right hand side, we have the surface loads:

\mathcal{G}^{h}(\boldsymbol{G}^{h})(\boldsymbol{v})=\int_{\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\}}\boldsymbol{G}^{h}\boldsymbol{v}\,d\widehat{x},\qquad\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{3}),

satisfying

\displaystyle\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{G}^{h}\subset H^{1}(0,T,L^{2}\bigl(\omega\times\bigl\{-1/2,1/2\bigr\};{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\bigr))\textrm{ is bounded},

(3.123)

\displaystyle\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{G}^{h}\xrightharpoonup{t,2-r,2}\boldsymbol{G}\in H^{1}\Bigl(0,T;L^{2}\bigl(\omega\times\bigl\{-1/2,1/2\bigr\};{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\bigr)\Bigr).

(3.124)

Instead of the condition (
2.40
), we demand that for every
t>0
we have

\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}(t)\,dx+\int_{\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\}}\boldsymbol{G}^{h}(t)\,d\widehat{x}=0.

In the limit equations we then obtain the following terms on the right hand side of (
3.58
)

\int_{\omega}\boldsymbol{G}\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta}\,d\widehat{x}+\int_{\omega}\left(\boldsymbol{G}(\cdot,-1/2)-\boldsymbol{G}(\cdot,1/2)\right)\cdot\nabla_{\widehat{x}}\theta_{3}\,d\widehat{x}.

Under the assumption on strong convergence, the strong convergence of the solutions can also be proved in the spirit of Section
3.5
. Note also that the existence and uniqueness from Section
3.4
can be used.

3.6.4
Fluid part intersects the upper and lower boundary

In cases where condition (
2.2
) is not met, it becomes necessary to enforce boundary conditions for the fluid component at the upper and lower boundary. While incorporating surface loads on the elastic component presents no issue, specifying boundary conditions for the fluid poses a challenge.

In
[
13
]
, various boundary conditions and their corresponding equations in the weak formulation for the Stokes problem are discussed. It’s important to note that non-homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions cannot be directly imposed on the upper and lower boundary for the fluid. This limitation arises due to the necessity of employing a special scaling that we used to derive the limiting equations.

Under this scaling, it follows that
\boldsymbol{u}_{f}
is of the order of
h^{2}
. Here,
\boldsymbol{u}_{f}
doesn’t represent the actual position of the fluid (which is given by
\boldsymbol{u}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}+\boldsymbol{u}^{f}
), and the scaling of the fluid’s position consequently differs (being of order one in the third component). Consequently, the imposition of non-homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions on the upper and lower boundary is not possible.

As an alternative, one can only apply artificial Dirichlet boundary conditions on these boundaries, taking the form:

\boldsymbol{u}_{f}=h^{2}\boldsymbol{g}\textrm{ on }\partial\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\}\cap\Omega^{h}_{f},\quad\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{1}(0,T;V^{h})\cap H^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})).

As a consequence of the derivation (
3.40
) we would obtain the additional term of the form

\int_{\omega}\left(g_{3}(\cdot,1/2)\cdot\varphi(\cdot,1/2)-g_{3}(\cdot,-1/2)\cdot\varphi(\cdot,-1/2)\right)\,d\widehat{x}

on the right hand side of (
3.59
).
Section
3.4
also includes the existence and uniqueness result for this case. However, due to the real Dirichlet condition for velocity being
\boldsymbol{u}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}+\boldsymbol{u}_{f}
, where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}
denotes the extension from the solid part, and considering that prescribing deformation for the solid part at the upper and lower boundaries isn’t feasible (thus surface loads are applied), this scenario necessitates a thorough analysis concerning the solution’s existence and
a priori
estimates for the microscopic solution.

Additionally, the other boundary conditions discussed in
[
13
]
involve aspects of the fluid velocity (i.e. position) and pressure, encountering a similar issue as with the Dirichlet boundary condition. Nonetheless, if these conditions are imposed on
\boldsymbol{u}_{f}
, it becomes feasible to address the subsequent condition:

(u_{f})_{\alpha}=h^{2}g_{\alpha},\quad p=p_{l},\textrm{ on }\partial\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\}\cap\Omega^{h}_{f},

(3.125)

The impact of condition (
3.125
) on the weak formulation is addressed in
[
13
]
. This involves the introduction of additional terms on the right-hand side of (
2.27
), consequently leading to the emergence of further terms on the right-hand side of (
3.58
). Once more, this necessitates preliminary analysis regarding the solution’s existence and
a priori
estimates for the microscopic problem.

Generally, it is difficult to justify from the microscopic point of view, any other condition besides the condition
\partial_{3}p=0
at the upper and lower boundary.

4
The problem with inertial term

We consider equation (
2.27
) with
\eta=\eta(h)=1
. Following the structure laid out in Section
3
, we maintain a similar approach but omit the corresponding proofs.

In Section
4.1
, we present the compactness result and formulate the limit problem. Subsequently, in Section
4.2
, we establish both the existence and uniqueness of the solution, along with demonstrating an energy-type equality for the limit problem.
In
[
21
, Appendix B]
it is already noted that the semigroup approach can be helpful in obtaining the existence result. However, here again an additional effort needs to be done to define the appropriate operators, since the limit equations do not decouple, see the proof of Proposition
4.7
below). In the end, we will obtain the existence result by using Galerkin approximation, but we will use semigroup approach to obtain the appropriate approximation of the solution, which we will use to prove energy-type equality in the similar way as it was done in quasi-static case.
Finally, in Section
4.3
, we articulate the result regarding strong convergence.
The following set will be of importance: For given
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{1}_{\#}(\omega)
we denote

L_{\kappa,\mathfrak{b}}:=\left\{\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}_{\#}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}):\int_{\omega}\bar{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\psi}=-\int_{\Omega}\kappa\nabla_{\hat{x}}\mathfrak{b}\,d\hat{x}\right\}.

Note that for any
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{1}_{\#}(\omega)
we have
L_{1}\equiv L_{1,\mathfrak{b}}=\dot{H}^{1}_{\#}(\omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. Furthermore, we introduce
N:=H^{2}_{\#}(\omega)
and recall
M:=L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(J_{K})\oplus L^{2}(J_{p}\backslash J_{K}))
.

4.1
Compactness and limit equations

The following theorem can be proved in the same way as Theorem
3.1
and Theorem
3.7
.
It provides compactness statement and establishes the limit problem. We will state it without proof.

(4.4)

Remark 4.2
.

In the limit one obtains partially quasi-static model (there is no inertia term for the part of in-plane component
\mathfrak{a}
). This also happens in the case of elastic plate (see
[
9
]
). The non-decoupling of bending (inertial) and membrane (quasi-static) equations also happens in elasticity when there are heterogeneities across thickness, see
[
8
]
. As already mentioned, although the 3D evolution equations derived in
[
12
]
have memory effects, the 2D equations obtained here do not have memory effects. This is the consequence of (implicit) scaling of time that is done to obtain the limit model (this scaling of time is also done in linearized elasticity to obtain the plate models, see
[
9
,
8
]
). However, in
[
8
]
also the models obtained without scaling of time (they are called membrane models) are discussed.

4.2
Analysis of the limit equations

Again, we can assume that
\mathfrak{a}\in L_{1}

\mathfrak{a}\in L_{\kappa}

The following theorem gives us uniqueness of solution of (

)-(

).

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{b}_{0}\in N
,
\mathfrak{b}_{1}\in L^{2}(\omega)
,

,
\boldsymbol{F}_{1}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{\prime}_{1})
,
\boldsymbol{F}_{2}\in L^{2}(0,T;N^{\prime})
,
\boldsymbol{G}\in L^{2}(0,T;M^{\prime})
. The solution of (
4.5
)-(
4.7
), if it exists, is unique.

Proof.

We assume that
\boldsymbol{F}_{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{G}=0
, for
\alpha=1,2
,
\mathfrak{b}_{0}=\mathfrak{b}_{1}=0
,
t_{0}=0
in (
4.5
)-(
4.7
) and for fixed
0<T^{\prime}<T
take the following test functions in (
4.6
)

\theta_{3}=\begin{cases}\int_{T^{\prime}}^{t}\mathfrak{b}(s)\,ds,&\textrm{ for all }0\leq t<T^{\prime},\\
0,&\textrm{ for all }T^{\prime}\leq t\leq T.\end{cases}\quad\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}=\begin{cases}\int_{T^{\prime}}^{t}\mathfrak{a}(s)\,ds,&\textrm{ for all }0\leq t<T^{\prime}\\
0,&\textrm{ for all }T^{\prime}\leq t\leq T.\end{cases},

and in (
4.7
)

\varphi(t)=\begin{cases}\int_{T^{\prime}}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}p(r)\,dr\,ds,&\textrm{ for all }0\leq t<T^{\prime},\\
0,&\textrm{ for all }T^{\prime}\leq t\leq T.\end{cases}

We have that

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\bar{\kappa}(\widehat{x})\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}\partial_{t}\theta_{3}\,dx\,dt=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\omega}\bar{\kappa}(\widehat{x})\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\frac{d}{dt}\mathfrak{b}^{2}\,dt\,d\widehat{x}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\omega}\bar{\kappa}(\widehat{x})\mathfrak{b}^{2}(T^{\prime})\,d\widehat{x},

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\theta_{3})\,d\widehat{x}\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}\left(e_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{T^{\prime}}^{t}\mathfrak{a}(s)\,ds),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{T^{\prime}}^{t}\mathfrak{b}(s)\,ds)\right):\left(e_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{T^{\prime}}^{t}\mathfrak{a}(s)\,ds),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{T^{\prime}}^{t}\mathfrak{b}(s)\,ds)\right)d\widehat{x}\,dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}\left(e_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\mathfrak{a}(s)\,ds),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\mathfrak{b}(s)\,ds)\right):\left(e_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\mathfrak{a}(s)\,ds),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\mathfrak{b}(s)\,ds)\right)d\widehat{x},

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))p\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}))\right]\,d\widehat{x}\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)\frac{d}{dt}\int_{0}^{t}p(s)\,ds\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(e_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{T^{\prime}}^{t}\mathfrak{a}(s)\,ds))\right]\,d\widehat{x}\,dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)\int_{0}^{t}p\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}))\right]\,d\widehat{x}\,dt,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)

\displaystyle x_{3}p\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\theta_{3})\right]\,d\widehat{x}\,dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)\int_{0}^{t}p\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b})\right]\,d\widehat{x}\,dt,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})p\,\partial_{t}\varphi dx\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\int_{\Omega}M_{0}(x_{3})p\int_{0}^{t}pdx\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})\left(\int_{0}^{t}p\right)^{2}dx\,dt=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}M_{0}(x_{3})\left(\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}p\right)^{2}\,dx,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}p\,\partial_{3}\varphi dx\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{t}\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{3}p\,\int_{T^{\prime}}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\partial_{3}p(r)\,dr\,ds\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}\int_{0}^{t}p\,\partial_{3}\int_{0}^{t}p\,dx\,dt,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)\int_{0}^{t}p\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}))\right]\,d\widehat{x}\,dt,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)

\displaystyle x_{3}\partial_{t}\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}\right)\,d\widehat{x}\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)\int_{0}^{t}p\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b})\right]\,d\widehat{x}\,dt.

Multiplying both (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) with
-1
and adding them we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\omega}\bar{\kappa}(\widehat{x})\mathfrak{b}^{2}(T^{\prime})\,d\widehat{x}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}\left(e_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\mathfrak{a}(s)\,ds),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\mathfrak{b}(s)\,ds)\right):\left(e_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\mathfrak{a}(s)\,ds),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}(\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\mathfrak{b}(s)\,ds)\right)d\widehat{x}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}M_{0}(x_{3})\left(\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}p\right)^{2}\,dx+\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}\int_{0}^{t}p\,\partial_{3}\int_{0}^{t}p\,dx\,dt=0.

Since this is valid for arbitrary
0\leq T^{\prime}\leq T
we obtain
\mathfrak{b}=p=0
on
[0,T]
. To conclude uniqueness of
\mathfrak{a}
we use (
4.6
) with
\theta_{3}=0
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}
arbitrary and the fact that
\mathfrak{a}\in L_{1}
. This proves uniqueness.
∎

Before proving the existence result, we first recall some results from operator theory. Let
(H,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H})
be a separable Hilbert space. Let
\mathcal{A}:H\to H
be a generator of the strongly continuous semigroup
e^{t\mathcal{A}}
,
\|e^{t\mathcal{A}}\|_{H\to H}\leq M_{\mathcal{A}}e^{\omega_{\mathcal{A}}t}
,
t\geq 0
, where
M_{\mathcal{A}}>0
is a positive constant and
\omega_{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Here
\|\cdot\|_{H\to H}
denotes the operator norm for the bounded operators from
H
to
H
.
For
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{1}(0,T;H)
, consider the following evolution problem:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t)=\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{u}(t)+\boldsymbol{f}(t),&\\
\boldsymbol{u}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in H.&\end{cases}

(4.8)

Definition 4.4
.

We call
\boldsymbol{u}\in C(0,T;H)
a mild solution of (
4.8
) if

{\boldsymbol{u}}(t)=e^{t\mathcal{A}}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}+\int_{0}^{T}e^{(t-s)\mathcal{A}}{\boldsymbol{f}}(s)ds.

(4.9)

Remark 4.5
.

Under the condition that
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
and
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{p}(0,T;\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}))
we have that the mild solution is the strong solution of (
4.8
) in
W^{1,p}(0,T;H)\cap C(0,T;\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}))
(recall that since
\mathcal{A}
is closed operator,
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is a Hilbert space with graph norm).

The following lemma gives us a stability estimate.

Lemma 4.6
.

We have that the mild solution of (
4.8
) satisfies:

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H)}\leq M_{\mathcal{A}}e^{\omega_{\mathcal{A}}T}\left(\|{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}\|_{H}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{1}(0,T;H)}\right).

(4.10)

Proof.

The claims follows directly from (
4.9
).
∎

Before proving the existence result we will show that the system (
4.5
)-(
4.7
) can be put formally in the form of (
4.8
) (with
\mathcal{A}
generator of the contraction semigroup on appropriate Hilbert space
H
). Although from this we will not be able to conclude the existence of the mild solution for (
4.5
)-(
4.7
) (since the loads are not regular enough) it will give us some information on the system which we will use to prove energy-type equality. Also this will enable us to obtain the existence of the solution for each step of Galerkin approximation.

Proposition 4.7
.

There exists a Hilbert space
(H,\langle\cdot\rangle_{H})
and a Hilbert space
(V,\langle\cdot\rangle_{V})
densely embedded in
H
such that the system (
4.5
)-(
4.7
) can be put in the form (
4.8
) for
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
and where
\mathcal{A}
is a generator of a strongly continuous semigroup on
H
.

Proof.

Step 1
.
Definition of
H
.
We define

H:=N\times L^{2}(\omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega_{p}),

(4.11)

endowed with the scalar product

\langle(\mathfrak{b}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{1},p_{1})^{T},(\mathfrak{b}_{2},\mathfrak{v}_{2},p_{2})^{T}\rangle_{\tilde{b},H}=\fint_{\omega}\mathfrak{b}_{1}\cdot\fint_{\omega}\mathfrak{b}_{2}+a_{1}(\mathfrak{b}_{1},\mathfrak{b}_{2})+\langle\bar{\kappa}\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\omega)}+\widetilde{b}(p_{1},p_{2}),

where
a_{1}
is a bilinear form on
H^{2}_{\#}(\omega)
is defined by:

a_{1}(\mathfrak{b}_{1},\mathfrak{b}_{2})=\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\mathfrak{b}_{1}}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{1}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\mathfrak{b}_{2}}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{2})d\widehat{x}.

(4.12)

Here
\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}}\in L_{1}
for
\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}\in N
is a unique solution of

\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}),0)d\widehat{x}=0,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\in L_{1}.

(4.13)

\widetilde{b}
is a bilinear form on
L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
defined by

\widetilde{b}(p_{1},p_{2})=\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}p_{1},p_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})},

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}:L^{2}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\Omega)
is a positive definite bounded operator defined by bilinear form

\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}p_{1},p_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}=\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})p_{1}p_{2}\,dx+\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))p_{2}\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{p_{1}}\right)\right)d\widehat{x},

(4.14)

and
\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{\widehat{p}}\in L_{1}
for
\widehat{p}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
is the unique solution of

\begin{split}&\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{\widehat{p}}),0):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}),0)d\widehat{x}=\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)\widehat{p}\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}))\right]\,d\widehat{x},\\
&\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\in\dot{H}^{1}_{\#}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}).\end{split}

(4.15)

From (
4.14
) and (
4.15
) it follows that

\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}p_{1},p_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}=\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})p_{1}p_{2}\,dx+\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{p_{1}}),0):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{p_{2}}),0)d\widehat{x},

(4.16)

from which we have positive definiteness. We denote the corresponding norm by
\|\cdot\|_{\tilde{b},H}
.
We also endow
H
with the scalar product
which is given by

\langle(\mathfrak{b}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{1},p_{1})^{T},(\mathfrak{b}_{2},\mathfrak{v}_{2},p_{2})^{T}\rangle_{H}=\fint_{\omega}\mathfrak{b}_{1}\cdot\fint_{\omega}\mathfrak{b}_{2}+a_{1}(\mathfrak{b}_{1},\mathfrak{b}_{2})+\langle\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\omega)}+\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}.

The corresponding norm, denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{H}
, is equivalent to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\tilde{b},H}
. Note that if we denote by
\mathcal{B}:H\to H
the self adjoint continuous operator operator given by

\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}=(\mathfrak{b},\bar{\kappa}\mathfrak{v},\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}p)^{T},\quad\forall(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\in H,

(4.17)

then we have

\langle(\mathfrak{b}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{1},p_{1})^{T},(\mathfrak{b}_{2},\mathfrak{v}_{2},p_{2})^{T}\rangle_{\tilde{b},H}=\langle\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{b}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{1},p_{1})^{T},(\mathfrak{b}_{2},\mathfrak{v}_{2},p_{2})^{T}\rangle_{H},\quad\forall(\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{v}_{\alpha},p_{\alpha})^{T}\in H,\ \alpha=1,2,

(4.18)

from which it follows that

\|(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\|_{\tilde{b},H}=\|\mathcal{B}^{1/2}(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\|_{H}.

Step 2.

Definition of
\mathcal{A}
. We define formally the operator

on

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p\right)^{T}=\left(\mathfrak{v},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}_{1}}(\mathfrak{b},p)^{T},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}(\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\right)^{T},

(4.19)

where we formally put
24
24

24

At this point it is only formally since we did not specify the domain of
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
.

\begin{split}\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1}(\mathfrak{b},p)^{T},\widetilde{\theta}_{3}\rangle_{L^{2}(\omega)}=&-\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\mathfrak{b}}+\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{p}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}):(0,\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\widetilde{\theta}_{3})\,d\widehat{x}\\
&-\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}p\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\widetilde{\theta}_{3})\right]\,d\widehat{x},\quad\forall\widetilde{\theta}_{3}\in N.\end{split}

(4.20)

Here

For the definition of

\begin{split}&\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}(\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}=-\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\mathfrak{v}}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}\\
&+\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{v}\right)\,d\widehat{x}-\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}p\,\partial_{3}\varphi\,dx,\quad\forall\varphi\in M,\end{split}

(4.21)

where
\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\mathfrak{v}}\in L_{1}
is defined by (
4.13
). Next we want to show that (properly defined) modification of operator

. Firstly we want to show that the operator

(4.22)

From the first equation in the system (

(4.23)

Next we plug this into the second and third equation of (

We define the bilinear form on

in the following way:

\begin{split}a((\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T})=&\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\mathfrak{v}}+\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{p}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{v}):(0,\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\widetilde{\theta}_{3})\,d\widehat{x}\\
&+\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}p\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\widetilde{\theta}_{3})\right]\,d\widehat{x}\\

&+\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}p\,\partial_{3}\varphi\,dx.\end{split}

(4.24)

Note that the second and third equation of (

), taking into account (

\begin{split}\langle\mathfrak{v},\widetilde{\theta}_{3}\rangle_{L^{2}(\omega)}&+\langle p,\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}+a((\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T})=\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{2},\widetilde{\theta}_{3}\rangle_{L^{2}(\omega)}+\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{3},\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\\
&-\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{x}_{1}}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\boldsymbol{x}_{1}):(0,\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\widetilde{\theta}_{3})d\widehat{x},\ \ \forall(\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T}\in N\times M.\end{split}

(4.25)

As a consequence of the definition of

,

(4.26)

and also

(4.27)

Taking into account (

) and (

\begin{split}a((\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T})=&\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\mathfrak{v}},\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{v}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\widetilde{\theta}_{3}}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\widetilde{\theta}_{3})d\widehat{x}\\
&+\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\mathfrak{v}}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}\\

(4.28)

From this and Proposition
3.3
, one easily obtains that the form on the left hand side of (

) is coercive on

. Since it is obviously continuous, it follows from Lax-Milgram (non-symmetric case) that (

. Taking into account (

) we have that we can solve uniquely (

) for
\boldsymbol{x}\in H
. Next we define the operator

in the following way:

\circ

the domain of the operator

is given as the subset of

consisting of the solutions of (

) as

exhausts

. Obviously

(4.29)

\circ

for given

(4.30)

Note that

) and (

),(
4.21
) are valid for every
(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\in\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
,
\widetilde{\theta}_{3}\in N
,
\varphi\in M
.

Next, we define the operator
\mathcal{C}
,
H\ni\boldsymbol{y}\mapsto\mathcal{C}\boldsymbol{y}=(\mathfrak{b}_{1},\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T}\in H
, where
25
25

25

Below we will show that
\mathcal{C}=\left((I-\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}\right)^{T}
.

\mathfrak{b}_{1}=\boldsymbol{y}_{1}-\widetilde{\theta}_{3}+\fint_{\omega}\widetilde{\theta}_{3}\,d\hat{x},

(4.31)

and
(\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T}\in N\times M
are solutions of:

\begin{split}&\langle\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\mathfrak{v}\rangle_{L^{2}(\omega)}+\langle\varphi,p\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\widetilde{a}((\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T},(\mathfrak{v},p)^{T})=\langle\boldsymbol{y}_{2},\mathfrak{v}\rangle_{L^{2}(\omega)}+\langle\boldsymbol{y}_{3},p\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\\
&\hskip 4.30554pt+\fint_{\omega}\boldsymbol{y}_{1}\cdot\fint_{\omega}\mathfrak{v}+\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{y}_{1}}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\boldsymbol{y}_{1}):(0,\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{v})d\widehat{x},\quad\forall(\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\in N\times M.\end{split}

(4.32)

Here
\widetilde{a}
is the bilinear form on
N\times M
is defined in the following way

\widetilde{a}((\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T},(\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}):=a((\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T}),\quad\forall(\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T},(\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\in N\times M.

(4.33)

As a consequence of (
4.31
) and (
4.32
) we easily see that the operator
\mathcal{C}
has trivial kernel. To see this take
(\mathfrak{b}_{1},\widetilde{\theta}_{3},p)^{T}=0
in (
4.31
), (
4.32
) and conclude
\boldsymbol{y}=0
.
As a consequence of (
4.23
), (
4.25
),(
4.31
),(
4.32
) and (
4.33
) we have that

\langle(I-\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle_{H}=\langle\boldsymbol{x},\mathcal{C}\boldsymbol{y}\rangle_{H},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in H.

This means that
\mathcal{C}=\left((I-\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}\right)^{T}
.
The density of
\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
in
H
follows from the fact that the operator
\mathcal{C}
has trivial kernel, which implies that the range of the operator
(I-\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}
is dense in
H
.
Directly from the definition we see that the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
is closed. Namely, if
\boldsymbol{x}_{n}\xrightarrow{H}\boldsymbol{x}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\boldsymbol{x}_{n}\xrightarrow{H}\boldsymbol{y}
, we have the following:

\boldsymbol{x}\xleftarrow{H}\boldsymbol{x}_{n}=(I-\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}(I-\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})\boldsymbol{x}_{n}\xrightarrow{H}(I-\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}).

From this it follows that
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
and
(I-\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}
, which implies that
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{y}
. Furthermore, from (
4.20
), (
4.21
), (
4.23
) we obtain that for every
(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\in\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
we have

\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\rangle_{H}=\fint_{\omega}\mathfrak{b}\,d\widehat{x}\fint_{\omega}\mathfrak{v}\,d\widehat{x}-\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}p\,\partial_{3}p\,dx.

(4.34)

where we used (
4.26
) for
\widetilde{\theta}_{3}=\mathfrak{b}
and (
4.27
) for
\widetilde{\theta}_{3}=\mathfrak{v}
.
From (
4.17
) we conclude that there exists
c>0
such that

\langle(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}-c\mathcal{B})(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\rangle_{H}<0.

(4.35)

Obviously the operator

\mathcal{A}_{c}:=\mathcal{B}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}-cI:H\to H

(4.36)

(where
H
is equipped with either scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H}
or
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\widetilde{b},H})
is a closed, densely defined operator. From (
4.18
) and (
4.35
) we obtain for every
(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\in\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{c})

\langle(\mathcal{B}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}-cI)(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\rangle_{\tilde{b},H}=\langle(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}-c\mathcal{B})(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\rangle_{H}\leq 0.

(4.37)

From (
4.37
) we conclude that for every
(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\in\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
and
\lambda>0
we have

\langle(\lambda I-\mathcal{A}_{c})(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\rangle_{\tilde{b},H}\geq\lambda\|(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T}\|^{2}_{\tilde{b},H}.

(4.38)

From (
4.38
) we easily conclude that for every
\lambda>0
:

\textrm{Ker}(\lambda I-\mathcal{A}_{c})=\{0\}.

(4.39)

By repeating the same analysis for
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{T}
(note that the identity (
4.34
) is also valid for
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{T}
) we analogously conclude that
\textrm{Ker}(\lambda I-\mathcal{A}_{c})^{T}=\{0\},

where
(\cdot)^{T}
is for
\widetilde{A}
taken with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H}
while for
\lambda I-\mathcal{A}_{c}
is taken with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\tilde{b},H}
scalar product. This implies that the range of the operator
\lambda I-\mathcal{A}_{c}
is dense. From (
4.38
) we again conclude that the inverse of the operator
\lambda I-\mathcal{A}_{c}
can be extended to continuous operator and that

\|(\lambda I-\mathcal{A}_{c})^{-1}\|_{(\tilde{b},H)\to(\tilde{b},H)}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda},

(4.40)

where
\|\cdot\|_{(\tilde{b},H)\to(\tilde{b},H)}
denotes the operator norm taken with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\tilde{b},H}
norm on
H
. Using the closedness of the operator
\lambda I-\mathcal{A}_{c}
we conclude from (
4.40
) that the operator
\lambda I-\mathcal{A}_{c}
is surjective. Since by (
4.39
) it is also injective, it is bijective and (
4.40
) is valid. From Hille-Yosida theorem it follows that
\mathcal{A}_{c}
is a generator of a contraction semigroup.
Thus the operator
\mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{A}_{c}+cI=\mathcal{B}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
is a generator of a strongly continuous semigroup.

Step 3.

Definition of the loads and the initial pressure.
Obviously, from the construction the system (
4.5
)-(
4.7
) has the form (
4.8
) where

\begin{split}{}_{V^{\prime}}\langle\boldsymbol{f}(t),(\theta_{3},\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T}\rangle_{V}&={{}_{N^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{F}_{2},\theta_{3}\rangle_{N}-\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{3}^{\boldsymbol{F}_{1}}),0):(0,\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\theta_{3})d\widehat{x}+{{}_{M^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{G},\varphi\rangle_{M}\\
&-\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{3}^{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}_{1}(t)}\right)\right)d\widehat{x},\end{split}

(4.41)

and for
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}\in L^{\prime}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{a}_{3}^{\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}}\in L_{1}
is a unique solution of

\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{3}^{\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}}),0):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}),0)d\widehat{x}=\displaystyle{{}_{L_{1}^{\prime}}}\langle\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\rangle_{L_{1}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\in L_{1}.

(4.42)

The initial condition is given by

(\mathfrak{b}(0),\mathfrak{v}(0),p(0))^{\top}=(\mathfrak{b}_{0},\mathfrak{b}_{1},p_{0})^{\top},

(4.43)

where

\widetilde{b}(p_{0},h)=\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))h\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{3}^{\boldsymbol{F}_{1}(0)}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}+\int_{\Omega_{p}}t_{0}\cdot h\,dx,\quad\forall h\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p}).

(4.44)

To see this one needs to conclude from (

\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\mathfrak{b}}+\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{p}+\mathfrak{a}_{3}^{\boldsymbol{F}_{1}},

plug this in the equations (

) and (

), do integration by parts in time to move the time derivative from the test functions
\varphi
,
\theta_{3}
and introduce
\mathfrak{v}:=\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}
(this is standard when we want to write the second order problems as first order) and use (
4.14
), (

), (

)-(

)-(

) in the form:

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\theta_{3},\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T}\rangle_{\widetilde{b},H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{B}\partial_{t}(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\theta_{3},\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T}\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\theta_{3},\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T}\rangle_{H}+_{V^{\prime}}\langle\boldsymbol{f}(t),(\theta_{3},\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T}\rangle_{V}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{A}(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v},p)^{T},(\theta_{3},\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T}\rangle_{\widetilde{b},H}+_{V^{\prime}}\langle\boldsymbol{f}(t),(\theta_{3},\widetilde{\theta}_{3},\varphi)^{T}\rangle_{V},

with (
4.43
). This finishes the proof.
∎

The following theorem gives us existence of the solution of (
4.5
)-(
4.7
).

Theorem 4.8
.

If
\mathfrak{b}_{0}\in H^{2}_{\#}(\omega)
,
\mathfrak{b}_{1}\in L^{2}(\omega)
,
t_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
,
\boldsymbol{F}_{1}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{\prime}_{1})
,
\boldsymbol{F}_{2}\in L^{2}(0,T;N^{\prime})
,
\boldsymbol{G}\in L^{2}(0,T;M^{\prime})
, then there exists a solution
\mathfrak{a}\in L^{2}(0,T;L_{1})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in L^{2}(0,T;N)\cap H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega))
,
p\in L^{2}(0,T;M)
of (
4.2
)-(
4.4
). In addition we have that
\mathfrak{a}\in C([0,T];L_{1})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in C([0,T];N)\cap C^{1}([0,T];L^{2}(\omega))
,
p\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))
. Moreover there exists

C>0
such that

\begin{split}&\|\mathfrak{a}\|_{C(0,T;L_{1})}+\|\mathfrak{b}\|_{C(0,T;N)}+\|\mathfrak{b}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega))}+\|p\|_{C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}+\|p\|_{L^{2}(0,T;M)}\\
&\leq C\Big(\|\boldsymbol{F}_{1}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{\prime}_{1})}+\|\boldsymbol{F}_{2}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;N^{\prime})}+\|\boldsymbol{G}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;M^{\prime})}+\|\mathfrak{b}_{0}\|_{N}+\|\mathfrak{b}_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\omega)}+\|t_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\Big).\end{split}

(4.45)

Proof.

We use Galerkin approximation. We take a sequence of increasing finite dimensional subspaces
(G_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
G_{n}=G_{n}^{1}\times G_{n}^{2}\times G_{n}^{3}
where
G_{n}^{1}\subset L_{1}
,
G_{n}^{2}\subset N
,
G_{n}^{3}\subset M
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\overline{\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}G_{n}^{1}}=L_{1}
,
\overline{\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}G_{n}^{2}}=N
,
\overline{\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}G_{n}^{3}}=M
. We approximate the initial condition
\mathfrak{b}_{0}
with the sequence
(\mathfrak{b}_{0}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
G_{n}^{2}\ni\mathfrak{b}_{0}^{n}\to\mathfrak{b}_{0}
in N. We consider the solution of the problem: For
\mathfrak{b}_{1}\in L^{2}(\omega)
,
t_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
,
\boldsymbol{F}_{1}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{\prime}_{1})
,
\boldsymbol{F}_{2}\in L^{2}(0,T;N^{\prime})
,
\boldsymbol{G}_{1}\in H^{1}(0,T;M^{\prime})
,
\boldsymbol{G}_{2}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
we find
\mathfrak{a}_{n}\in H^{1}(0,T;G_{n}^{1})
,
\mathfrak{b}_{n}\in H^{2}(0,T;G_{n}^{2})
,
p_{n}\in H^{1}(0,T;G_{n}^{3})
which satisfy (
4.5
) with
\mathfrak{b}_{0}
replaced with
\mathfrak{b}_{0}^{n}
, (
4.6
) for every
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*},\theta_{3})\in H^{1}(0,T;G_{n}^{1}\times G_{n}^{2})
, and (
4.7
) for every
\varphi\in H^{1}(0,T;G_{n}^{3})
. The existence of such problem is guaranteed by the fact that the problem has the form (
4.8
) (see Definition
4.4
and Remark
4.5
), where
H=G_{n}^{2}\times G_{n}^{2}\times G_{n}^{3}
,
V=H
, and the operator
\mathcal{A}
is given by (matrix)
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n}
are defined with (
4.19
), (
4.20
), (
4.21
) and (
4.12
), (
4.17
), but restricted and with test functions on
G_{n}^{2}
and
G_{n}^{3}
respectively (we take
H
equipped with the scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\widetilde{b},H}
defined in the proof of Proposition
4.7
).
Since we are on a finite dimensional space the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
with
(
4.19
), (
4.20
) and (
4.21
) is not only formal.
Also
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B}_{n}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n})=G_{n}^{2}\times G_{n}^{2}\times G_{n}^{3}
and thus we have additional regularity in time of
\mathfrak{a}_{n},\mathfrak{b}_{n},p_{n}
. This enables us to modify (
4.5
)-(
4.7
) by doing integration by parts and conclude

\begin{split}\mathfrak{b}_{n}(0)=\mathfrak{b}_{0}^{n},\quad\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}_{n}(0)=\mathfrak{b}_{1}^{n},\quad p_{n}(0)=p_{0}^{n},\end{split}

(4.46)

\begin{split}&\int_{\omega}\bar{\kappa}(\widehat{x})\partial_{tt}\mathfrak{b}_{n}\theta_{3}\,d\widehat{x}+\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{n}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{n}):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\theta_{3})d\widehat{x}\\
&-\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)p_{n}\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(e_{\widehat{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}))\right]\,d\widehat{x}+\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}p\,dx_{3}:\left[\iota(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\theta_{3})\right]\,d\widehat{x}\\
&=\displaystyle{{}_{L^{\prime}_{1}}}\langle\boldsymbol{F}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\rangle_{L_{1}}+{{}_{N^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{F}_{2},\theta_{3}\rangle_{N},\quad\forall\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*},{\theta}_{3}\right)\in G_{n}^{1}\times G_{n}^{2},\textrm{ for a.e. t}\in(0,T);\end{split}

(4.47)

\begin{split}&\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})\partial_{t}p_{n}\,\varphi dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})\partial_{3}p_{n}\,\partial_{3}\varphi dx+\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\partial_{t}\mathfrak{a}_{n}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}\,dt\\
&-\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}\left(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3})\right)x_{3}\varphi\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}_{n}\right)\,d\widehat{x}={{}_{M^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{G},\varphi\rangle_{M}\quad\forall\varphi\in G_{n}^{3}\textrm{ for a.e. }t\in(0,T).\end{split}

(4.48)

Here
\mathfrak{b}_{1}^{n}
is the orthogonal projection of
\mathfrak{b}_{1}
on
G_{n}^{1}
with respect to the
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}(\omega)}
scalar product, while
p_{0}^{n}\in G_{n}^{3}
is given by the expression (cf. (
4.44
)):

\begin{split}\widetilde{b}(p_{0}^{n},h)=\int_{\omega}\int_{J_{p}}(|\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3})|\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{H}(x_{3}))h\,dx_{3}:\iota\left(e_{\widehat{x}}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{3}^{\boldsymbol{F}_{1}(0)}\right)\right)d\widehat{x}+\int_{\Omega_{p}}t_{0}\cdot h\,dx,\quad\forall h\in G_{n}^{3}.\end{split}

(4.49)

By testing then (
4.47
) with
(\partial_{t}\mathfrak{a}_{n},\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}_{n})
and (
4.48
) with
\varphi=p_{n}
and integrating over
(0,t)
for
t<T
we obtain

\begin{split}&\|\bar{\kappa}\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}_{n}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\omega)}+\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{n}(t)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{n}(t)):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{n}(t)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{n}(t))d\widehat{x}\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})p_{n}^{2}(t)\,dx+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})(\partial_{3}p_{n})^{2}\,dx\,d\tau=\|\bar{\kappa}\mathfrak{b}_{1}^{n}\|_{L^{2}(\omega)}\\
&\quad+\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{n}(0)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{n}(0)):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}_{n}(0)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}_{n}(0))d\widehat{x}+\int_{0}^{t}{{}_{L^{\prime}_{1}}}\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{n}\rangle_{L_{1}}\,d\tau\\
&\quad-{{}_{L^{\prime}_{1}}}\langle\boldsymbol{F}_{1}(0),\mathfrak{a}_{n}(0)\rangle_{L_{1}}+\int_{0}^{t}{{}_{N^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{F}_{2},\mathfrak{b}_{n}\rangle_{N}\,d\tau+\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})(p_{0}^{n})^{2}\,dx+\int_{0}^{t}{{}_{M^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{G},p_{n}\rangle_{M}\,d\tau.\\
\end{split}

(4.50)

To obtain the equation for
\mathfrak{a}_{n}(0)
from
\mathfrak{b}_{n}(0)
and
p_{n}(0)
one can test (
4.47
) with
\theta_{3}=0
for
t=0
. After doing Young’s inequality on the terms of the right side of equality sign of (
4.50
) and by using Proposition
3.3
one obtains the bound:

\begin{split}&\|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega))}+\|\mathfrak{a}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L_{1})}+\|\mathfrak{b}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;N)}+\|p_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{p}))}+\|p_{n}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;M)}\\
&\leq C\big(\|\boldsymbol{F}_{1}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{\prime}_{1})}+\|\boldsymbol{F}_{2}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;N^{\prime})}+\|\boldsymbol{G}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;M^{\prime})}+\|\mathfrak{b}_{0}^{n}\|_{N}+\|\mathfrak{b}_{1}^{n}\|_{L^{2}(\omega)}+\|p_{0}^{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\big),\end{split}

(4.51)

for some
C>0
.
From (
4.51
) we conclude that there exists weak limit

(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},p)\in\left(L^{\infty}(0,T;L)\cap W^{1,\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega))\right)\times L^{\infty}(0,T;N)\times\left(L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;M)\right)

of
(\mathfrak{a}_{n},\mathfrak{b}_{n},p_{n})
. By letting
n
to infinity in (
4.46
)-(
4.48
) (after writing them in the form of (
4.6
) and (
4.7
)) we obtain that
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},p)
satisfies (
4.5
)-(
4.7
). By letting
n
to infinity in (
4.51
) and by using (
4.49
) we obtain

\begin{split}&\|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega))}+\|\mathfrak{a}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L_{1})}+\|\mathfrak{b}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;N)}+\|p\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{p}))}+\|p\|_{L^{2}(0,T;M)}\\
&\leq C\big(\|\boldsymbol{F}_{1}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{\prime}_{1})}+\|\boldsymbol{F}_{2}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;N^{\prime})}+\|\boldsymbol{G}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;M^{\prime})}+\|\mathfrak{b}_{0}\|_{N}+\|\mathfrak{b}_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\omega)}+\|t_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{p})}\big).\end{split}

(4.52)

The additional regularity properties follow again from
[
14
, Remark 1, Chapter XVIII.5]
(cf. Remark
3.15
) and thus we can replace
L^{\infty}([0,T])
norms by
C([0,T]
on the left hand side of (
4.52
).
∎

Next we prove the energy-type identity, which is again needed to prove the strong convergence.

Proposition 4.9
.

If
\boldsymbol{F}_{1}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{\prime}_{1})
,
\boldsymbol{F}_{2}\in L^{2}(0,T;N^{\prime})
,
\boldsymbol{G}\in L^{2}(0,T;M^{\prime})
,
\mathfrak{b}_{0}\in N
,
\mathfrak{b}_{1}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
,
t_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
\mathfrak{a}\in C(0,T;L_{1})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in C(0,T;N)\cap H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega))
,
p\in L^{2}(0,T;M)\cap C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
is the (unique) solution of (
4.46
)-(
4.48
), then we have for every
t\in[0,T]
:

\begin{split}&\|\bar{\kappa}\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\omega)}+\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}(t)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(t)):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}(t)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(t))d\widehat{x}\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})p^{2}(t)\,dx+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{p}}\mathbb{K}_{33}(x_{3})(\partial_{3}p)^{2}\,dx\,d\tau=\|\bar{\kappa}\mathfrak{b}_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\omega)}\\
&\quad+\int_{\omega}\mathbb{A}^{\rm hom}(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}(0)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(0)):(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a}(0)),\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(0))d\widehat{x}+\int_{0}^{t}{{}_{L_{1}^{\prime}}}\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{F}_{1},\mathfrak{a}\rangle_{L_{1}}\,d\tau\\
&\quad-{{}_{L_{1}^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{F}_{1}(0),\mathfrak{a}(0)\rangle_{L_{1}}+\int_{0}^{t}{{}_{N^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{F}_{2},\mathfrak{b}\rangle_{N}\,d\tau+\int_{\Omega_{p}}M_{0}(x_{3})(p_{0})^{2}\,dx+\int_{0}^{t}{{}_{M^{\prime}}}\langle\boldsymbol{G},p\rangle_{M}\,d\tau.\end{split}

(4.53)

Here
p_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{p})
is defined by (
4.44
).

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{a}\in H^{1}(0,T;L_{1})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{1}(0,T;N)\cap H^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega))
and
p\in L^{2}(0,T;M)\cap H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
we would obtain (
4.53
) in the same way as we obtained (
4.50
). For the general case we use approximation. We know that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
, where
\mathcal{A}
is defined in the proof of Proposition
4.7
, is dense in
H
defined by (
4.11
) and
H
is dense in
V^{\prime}
, where
V
is defined by (
4.29
). From this we also have that
L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}))
is dense in
L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
. The claim for general
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},p)
follows by approximation argument by approximating with
\boldsymbol{F}_{2}
,
\boldsymbol{G}
such that
\boldsymbol{f}
defined by (
4.41
) is in
L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}))
and approximating
\mathfrak{b}_{0},\mathfrak{b}_{1},t_{0}
such that the initial conditions (
4.43
) is in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
.
By using Remark
4.5
we know that the strong solution of (
4.8
) has the above required regularity (with this approximated loads and initial condition) and solves (
4.46
)-(
4.48
) and thus satisfies (
4.53
). The claim for the original loads follows then by using stability estimate (
4.45
).
∎

4.3
Strong convergence

We will state the convergence result which is analogous to Theorem
3.22
and Theorem
3.23
. The proof goes in an analogous way and we will skip it.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let assumptions (
2.40
) and (
2.41
) be satisfied, together with the Assumptions
2.2
. Furthermore, we assume that
\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{F}^{h}\xrightarrow{t,2-r,2}\boldsymbol{F},\quad h\partial_{t}F_{\alpha}^{h}\xrightarrow{t,2-r,2}\partial_{t}F_{\alpha},\textrm{ for }\alpha=1,2,

where
\boldsymbol{F}\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{2}))\times L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y}))
and

F_{\alpha}(0)=0
, for
\alpha=1,2
.
Let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{h},p^{h})
be the solution of (
2.27
) with initial conditions (
2.28
). Than, the convergences (
3.104
) and (
3.105
) are valid as well as

\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\kappa^{h}(x)|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\omega}\bar{\kappa}(\widehat{x})\left|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}\right|^{2}\,d\widehat{x}\,dt.

Furthermore, convergences (
3.121
) and (
3.122
) are valid as well as:

\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{h}(t)-(0,0,\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}(t))^{T}|^{2}\,dx\,dt=0.

Here
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},p)\in L^{2}(0,T;L_{\kappa,\mathfrak{b}})\times\left(L^{2}(0,T;N)\cap H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\omega))\right)\times L^{2}(0,T;M)
are solutions of (
4.2
)-(
4.4
) and
\boldsymbol{u}^{f}_{0}\in H^{1}(0,T;

L^{2}(\Omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}_{f}(x_{3});\mathbb{R}^{3})))
is defined with (
3.79
).

Appendix

Appendix A
Griso’s decomposition and its consequences

In this section we use Griso’s decomposition to characterize the sequences with bounded symmetrized scaled gradients. These results present fundamental tools to deal with dimension reduction problems, since in these problems, in the
a priori
estimates, one only has the boundedness of symmetrized scaled gradients, which has to be combined with boundary conditions (and we show how they can be combined with Dirichlet or periodic boundary conditions).
The analogue results were shown in
[
30
,
6
,
8
]
. Here we need some additional claims where we deal with periodic boundary conditions.

Griso’s decomposition tells us how on
h
level we can decompose sequences with bounded elastic energy (on thin domain) and gives estimate of the reminder. This result has to be combined with certain compactness statements with respect two-scale convergence to fully characterize possible two-scale limits of sequences of symmetrized scaled gradients.

Let
\omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be an open set and let us denote
\Omega=\omega\times I
.
Let
\gamma_{D}\subset\partial\omega
be of positive measure and let
\Gamma_{D}=\gamma_{D}\times I
. We denote by
H^{1}_{\Gamma_{D}}(\Omega)
the subspace of
H^{1}(\Omega)
containing functions with zero trace on
\Gamma_{D}
. Analogously we define
H^{1}_{\gamma_{D}}(\omega)
. Similarly,
H^{2}_{\gamma_{D}}(\omega)
denotes the subspace of
H^{2}(\omega)
such that the trace of the functions and its first derivatives on
\gamma_{D}
is zero.

First we state Griso’s decomposition. Although in
[
20
]
it is stated on thin domain, we state the result here on the canonical domain.

Theorem A.1

(Griso’s decomposition,
[
20
]
, Theorem 2.3)
.

Let
\omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be a set with Lipschitz boundary and
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}(\Omega,{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
. Then for arbitrary
h\in(0,1)

the following identity holds

\boldsymbol{\psi}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(\widehat{x})+\boldsymbol{r}(\widehat{x})\wedge x_{3}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\widehat{\psi}_{1}(\widehat{x})+r_{2}(\widehat{x})x_{3}+\overline{\psi}_{1}(x)\\
\widehat{\psi}_{2}(\widehat{x})-r_{1}(\widehat{x})x_{3}+\overline{\psi}_{2}(x)\\
\widehat{\psi}_{3}(\widehat{x})+\overline{\psi}_{3}(x)\end{array}\right.\,,

(A.1)

where

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(\widehat{x})=\int_{I}\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{x},x_{3}){\mathrm{d}}x_{3}\,,\quad\boldsymbol{r}(x^{\prime})=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}12\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{I}x_{3}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\wedge\boldsymbol{\psi}(\widehat{x},x_{3}){\mathrm{d}}x_{3}\,.

(A.2)

Moreover, the following inequality holds

\|e_{h}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+\boldsymbol{r}\wedge x_{3}\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}^{2}+\|\nabla_{h}\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}^{2}+\frac{1}{h^{2}}\|\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}\leq C\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\psi})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}^{2}\,,

(A.3)

with constant
C>0
depending only on
\omega
.

Remark A.2
.

We have that

\begin{split}\|e_{h}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+\boldsymbol{r}\wedge x_{3}\boldsymbol{e}_{3})&\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}^{2}=\|e_{\widehat{x}}(\widehat{\psi}_{1},\widehat{\psi}_{2})\|_{L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}+\frac{1}{12}\|e_{\widehat{x}}(r_{2},-r_{1})\|_{L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}\\
&+\frac{1}{h^{2}}\|\partial_{1}(h\widehat{\psi}_{3})+r_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\omega)}^{2}+\frac{1}{h^{2}}\|\partial_{2}(h\widehat{\psi}_{3})-r_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\omega)}^{2}.\end{split}

(A.4)

The following lemma is a direct consequence of Griso’s decomposition and is proven in
[
6
, Lemma A4]
.
It decomposes the sequence which has bounded symmetrized scaled gradients in the ”limit deformation” that follows Kirchoff-Love ansatz and the remainder, which is further characterized in Lemma
A.5
.

Lemma A.3
.

Consider a bounded set
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with Lipschitz boundary. Suppose that
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})_{h\in(0,1)}\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{h\to 0}\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}<\infty.

Then, there exists a subsequence (still labeled by
h>0
) such that

\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}=(\mathfrak{a}_{1}-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{a}_{2}-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b},h^{-1}\mathfrak{b})^{T}+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h},

(A.5)

for some
\mathfrak{a}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{D}}(\omega)
and a sequence
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h})_{h\in(0,1)}\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which satisfies
h\pi_{1/h}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}\overset{L^{2}}{\to}0
.
In particular,

e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})=\iota(e_{\widehat{x}}(\mathfrak{a})-x_{3}\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\mathfrak{b})+e_{h}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}).

Remark A.4
.

For the case when
\omega
is rectangle and
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})_{h\in(0,1)}\subset H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
satisfies that
h\int_{\Omega}\pi_{1/h}(\kappa^{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})
is bounded for some
(\kappa^{h})_{h\in(0,1)}\subset L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, which is uniformly bounded (independently of
h
) from below and above by a positive constant, we have that
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{2}_{\#}(\omega)
,
\mathfrak{a}\in H^{1}_{\#}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h})_{h\in(0,1)}\subset H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
. This can be shown following the proof of Lemma
A.3
and using the result of Proposition
A.7
.

The following is a part of
[
8
, Lemma A10]
. Together with Lemma
A.3
it gives the desired compactness result for the sequence with bounded symmetrized scaled gradients, which is stronger than the one that would follow from Theorem
A.1
.
However, it requires
C^{1,1}
regularity of the domain (or periodic boundary conditions, see Remark
A.6
below). That is the reason why we still needed Theorem
A.1
in
[
29
,
6
,
8
]

and combined it with Lemma
A.5
on
C^{1,1}
regular subsets of Lipschitz regular domain.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a connected set with
C^{1,1}
boundary. If
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is such that

h\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\overset{L^{2}}{\to}0,\quad\displaystyle\limsup_{h\to 0}\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq M<\infty,

(A.6)

then there exist
(\varphi^{h})_{h>0}\subset H^{2}(\omega)
,
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that

e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})=-x_{3}\iota(\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{x}}\varphi^{h})+e_{h}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h})+o^{h},

(A.7)

where
o^{h}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
is such that
o^{h}\overset{L^{2}}{\to}0
. In addition, the following properties hold:

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\left(\|\varphi^{h}\|_{H^{1}(\omega)}+\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\right)=0,\quad\displaystyle\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(\|\varphi^{h}\|_{H^{2}(\omega)}+\|\nabla_{h}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\right)\leq CM,

(A.8)

where
C
depends only on
\omega
.

Remark A.6
.

In the case when
\omega
is a rectangle and
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})_{h>0}\subset H_{\#}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the statement of Lemma
A.5
holds with
(\varphi^{h})_{h\in(0,1)}\subset H_{\#}^{2}(\omega)
,
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h})_{h\in(0,1)}\subset H_{\#}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
. This can be proved following the proof of
[
8
, Lemma A10]
.

Using Theorem
A.1
we prove the following statement. It tells us that the components of the sequence with bounded symmetrized scaled gradients that satisfy periodic boundary condition, after appropriately scaled, are bounded in
H^{1}
norm.
This scaling is standard in the context of dimension reduction problems in linearized elasticity.
Moreover, in order to obtain
H^{1}
bondedness of the scaled components, in addition one only needs to have the control on the scaled weighted mean value of the components.

Proposition A.7
.

Let
\omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be a rectangle. Let
(\kappa^{h})_{h\in(0,1)}\subset L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, be such that
c_{0}<\kappa^{h}<c_{1}
almost everywhere, for some
c_{0},\,c_{1}>0
independent of
h
. Then there exists
C>0
such that for every
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})_{h\in(0,1)}\subset H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
we have

\|\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{h}\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\left|\int_{\Omega}\pi_{1/h}(\kappa^{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})\right|\right).

(A.9)

Proof.

We assume the opposite, that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a sequence
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,1)
and
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H^{1}_{\#}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that

\|\pi_{1/h_{n}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h_{n}}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\geq n\left(\frac{1}{h_{n}}\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h_{n}})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\left|\int_{\Omega}\pi_{1/h_{n}}(\kappa^{h_{n}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h_{n}})\right|\right).

(A.10)

Without a loss of generality, we can assume that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\|\pi_{1/h_{n}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h_{n}}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}=1,\quad\frac{1}{h_{n}}\|e_{h}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h_{n}})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\left|\int_{\Omega}\pi_{1/h_{n}}(\kappa^{h_{n}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h_{n}})\right|\leq\frac{1}{n}.

(A.11)

We decompose
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}
according to (
A.1
), noting that the decomposition satisfies the properties (
A.2
) and (
A.3
). Using (
A.4
), periodicity and (
A.11
) we conclude that

\left|\int_{\omega}\boldsymbol{r}^{h_{n}}\right|\leq\frac{Ch_{n}^{2}}{n}.

(A.12)

By using (
A.12
), Korn’s inequality for periodic functions (see Lemma
A.8
below), we conclude from (
A.4
) that

\frac{1}{h_{n}}\|\boldsymbol{r}^{h_{n}}\|_{H^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\frac{C}{n}.

(A.13)

From (
A.13
) we conclude that
\frac{\boldsymbol{r}^{h_{n}}}{h_{n}}\to 0
strongly in
H^{1}(\omega)
.

From the decomposition (
A.1
), using (
A.3
), (
A.11
) and (
A.13
) we conclude that

\left|\int_{\omega}\pi_{1/h_{n}}(\overline{\kappa}^{h_{n}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h_{n}})\right|\leq\frac{C}{n},

(A.14)

where
\overline{\kappa}^{h_{n}}=\int_{I}\kappa^{h_{n}}\,dx_{3}
.
Using (
A.14
) and again (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) and Korn’s inequality for periodic functions (Lemma
A.8
below) for
(\widehat{\psi}_{1}^{h_{n}},\widehat{\psi}_{2}^{h_{n}})
and Poincare inequality for
\widehat{\psi}_{3}^{h_{n}}
we conclude

\left\|\pi_{1/h_{n}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h_{n}}-\fint_{\omega}\pi_{1/h_{n}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h_{n}}\right\|_{H^{1}(\omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{3})}\leq\frac{C}{n},

(A.15)

We denote by
C_{n}=\fint_{\omega}\pi_{1/h_{n}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h_{n}}
. From (
A.15
) it follows that
\pi_{1/h_{n}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h_{n}}-C_{n}\to 0
, strongly in
L^{2}(\omega)
. From (
A.14
), using the boundedness of
\overline{\kappa}^{h_{n}}
we conclude that
that
C_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

It follows that
\pi_{1/h_{n}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h_{n}}\to 0
, strongly in
H^{1}(\omega)
. Taking into account (
A.1
), (
A.3
), (
A.11
) and (
A.13
) this contradicts the fact that
\|\pi_{1/h_{n}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h_{n}}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}=1
.
∎

The following lemma is the Korn’s inequality with periodic boundary condition. It is a direct consequence of
[
24
, Theorem 2.5]
. We will state it only for dimension two, although it is valid in arbitrary dimension.

Lemma A.8
.

Let
\omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be a rectangle. Then we have that there exists
C=C(\omega)
such that

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{H^{1}(\omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\leq C\left(\|e(\boldsymbol{u})\|_{L^{2}(\omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2})}+\left|\int_{\omega}\boldsymbol{u}\right|\right),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}_{\#}(\omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2}).

Remark A.9
.

As a trivial consequence of Lemma
A.8
we see that, if for
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}_{\#}(\omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
we have that
e(\boldsymbol{u})=0
, then we have
\boldsymbol{u}=C\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Appendix B
Auxiliary claims on two-scale convergence

In this section we prove some auxiliary claims on two-scale convergence. We assume
h>0
and
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(h)>0
are small parameters such that
\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\varepsilon(h)}{h}=0
. Unless otherwise stated we assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
with Lipschitz boundary. We take as before
Y=[0,1]^{3}
and

\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}^{3}/\mathbb{Z}^{3}
a unit flat torus and
\widehat{Y}=[0,1]^{2}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
(recall Section
1.2
).We give the following two definitions.

Definition B.1
.

Let
(u^{h})_{h>0}
be a bounded sequence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. We say that
u^{h}
weakly two-scale rescaled converges to
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y)
if

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u^{h}(x)\phi\left(x,\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\to\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{Y}u(x,y)\phi(x,y)\,dy\,dx\quad\forall\phi\in C_{c}(\Omega;C(\mathcal{Y})).

(B.1)

We denote this by
u^{h}\xrightharpoonup{2-r}u(x,y).

Furthermore, we say that
(u^{h})_{h>0}
strongly two-scale converges to
u\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y)
if

u^{h}\xrightharpoonup{2-r}u(x,y)
and
\|u^{h}\|_{L^{2}}\to\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times Y)}
.
We denote this by
u^{h}\xrightarrow{2-r}u(x,y).

The main motivation behind rescaled two-scale convergence in problem under consideration is that we always use the change of variables to transform the problem on the thin domain to the problem on the domain of unit thickness. In that way the cubes with
\varepsilon
side length become rectangular cuboid with length and width of size
\varepsilon
and height of size
\varepsilon/h
.

The standard two-scale weak and strong convergence we denote by
\xrightharpoonup{2}
and
\xrightarrow{2}
(for these notions see
[
2
]
). Note that if
\Omega=\omega\times I
, where
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
a set with Lipschitz boundary and if
(u^{h})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega)
, but depends only on
\hat{x}
, then the notion of weak two-scale convergence and weak two-scale rescaled convergence coincide. It is not difficult to check that the standard compactness statement for a sequence bounded in
L^{2}
is also valid in this case. The definition of two-scale rescaled convergence naturally extends to time dependent spaces.

Definition B.2
.

Let
(u^{h})_{h>0}
be a bounded sequence in
L^{p}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
, for
p\in(1,+\infty]
. We say that
(u^{h})_{h>0}
weakly two-scale rescaled converges to
u\in L^{p}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega\times Y))
if

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}u^{h}(x,t)\phi\left(x,\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\varphi(t)\,dx\,dt\to\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\int_{Y}u(t,x,y)\phi(x,y)\varphi(t)\,dy\,dx\,dt,

(B.2)

\forall\phi\in C_{c}(\Omega;C(\mathcal{Y})),\varphi\in C(0,T)
. We denote this by

u^{h}\xrightharpoonup{t,2-r,p}u(x,y,t).

Again the standard compactness statement for the sequence bounded in
L^{p}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
is also valid in this case.

For
p<\infty
, if in addition we have that
u^{h}(t)\xrightharpoonup{t,2-r,p}u(x,y,t)
for almost every
t\in[0,T]
and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|u^{h}\|^{p}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}dt\to\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{2}(\Omega\times Y)}\,dt,

(B.3)

then we will say that
(u^{h})_{\varepsilon>0}
strongly two-scale rescaled convergences to
u
and denote this by
u^{h}\xrightarrow{t,2-r,p}u(x,y,t).

We firstly state the following lemma which characterizes the two scale limits of Hessians. It is well known statement and its proof is e.g. the special case of
[
29
, Lemma 3]
.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
(\varphi^{h})_{h>0}\subset H^{2}(\omega)
be a bounded sequence. Assume that
\varphi^{h}\to\varphi_{0}
strongly in
L^{2}(\omega)
. Then there exists
\varphi_{1}\in L^{2}(\omega;\dot{H}^{2}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}))
such that on a subsequence we have

\nabla^{2}\varphi^{h}\xrightharpoonup{2}\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}(x)+\nabla^{2}_{\hat{y}}\varphi_{1}(x,\hat{y}).

(B.4)

We prove the following lemma and corollary which also characterizes two-scale limits of sequence that satisfies certain properties (the boundedness of the scaled gradients). The heuristic argument behind it goes via two-scale expansion, but the proof goes via duality argument.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\Omega=\omega\times I
, where
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
a bounded set with Lipschitz boundary and let
(\boldsymbol{V}^{h})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
be such that there exists
C>0
with

\|\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\varepsilon\|\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C.

(B.5)

Then there exists
\boldsymbol{\Psi}\in L^{2}(\Omega,H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
such that (up to a subsequence)

\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\xrightharpoonup{2-r}\boldsymbol{\Psi},\quad\varepsilon\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\xrightharpoonup{2-r}\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\Psi}.

(B.6)

Proof.

We know by two-scale compactness that there exist
\boldsymbol{\Psi}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\mathcal{V}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
such that
\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\xrightharpoonup{2-r}\boldsymbol{\Psi}
and
\varepsilon\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\xrightharpoonup{2-r}\mathcal{V}.

Let
v\in C_{c}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3})
be arbitrary. We have

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{V}(x,y)\cdot v(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(y)dydx&=\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\varepsilon\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}(x)\cdot v(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)dx\\
&=-\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}(x)\cdot v(x)\cdot\left(\partial_{y_{1}}\psi_{1}+\partial_{y_{2}}\psi_{2}+\partial_{y_{3}}\psi_{3}\right)dx\\
&\quad-\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}(x)\cdot\left(\varepsilon\partial_{1}v\psi_{1}+\varepsilon\partial_{2}v\psi_{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{h}\partial_{3}v\psi_{3}\right)dx\\
&=-\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\boldsymbol{\Psi}(x,y)\cdot v(x)\cdot\left(\partial_{y_{1}}\psi_{1}+\partial_{y_{2}}\psi_{2}+\partial_{y_{3}}\psi_{3}\right)\,dy\,dx\\
&=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\Psi}(x,y)\cdot v(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(y)\,dy\,dx.\end{split}

(B.7)

From this we have the claim.
∎

Corollary B.5
.

Let
p\in(1,+\infty]
,
\Omega=\omega\times I
, where
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
a bounded set with Lipschitz boundary and let
(\boldsymbol{V}^{h})_{h>0}\subset L^{p}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))
be such that there exists
C>0
with

\|\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\|_{L^{p}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}))}+\varepsilon\|\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\|_{L^{p}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}))}\leq C.

(B.8)

Then there exists
\boldsymbol{\Psi}\in L^{p}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega,H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3})))
such that (up to a subsequence)

\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\xrightharpoonup{t,2-r,p}\boldsymbol{\Psi},\quad\varepsilon\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\xrightharpoonup{t,2-r,p}\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\Psi}.

(B.9)

Proof.

The proof goes in an analogous way as the proof of Lemma
B.4
.
∎

The following lemma is in the same spirit as Lemma
B.4
. However, here one imposes the boundedness of the scaled gradients.

Lemma B.6
.

Let
\Omega=\omega\times I
, where
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
a bounded set with Lipschitz boundary and let
(\boldsymbol{V}^{h})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
be such that there exists
C>0
with

\|\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C.

(B.10)

There exist
\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}\in L^{2}(\omega,\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{R}^{3}))
such that (up to a subsequence)

\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\xrightharpoonup{2-r}\boldsymbol{V}:=\left(\nabla_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}|0\right)+\nabla_{y}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}+(0|0|\boldsymbol{V}_{1}).

(B.11)

Here
\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}
is the strong limit of
\boldsymbol{V}^{h}
in
L^{2}
(again on a subsequence).

Proof.

We know by two-scale compactness that there exists
\boldsymbol{V}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
such that
\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\xrightharpoonup{2-r}\boldsymbol{V}
, on a subsequence. Let
v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}=(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{1}|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{2}|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{3})\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
be such that
\operatorname{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
26
26

26

The divergence is taken per row
.
We compute

\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\boldsymbol{V}(x,y):\left(v(x)\boldsymbol{\psi}(y)\right)dydx=\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}(x):\left(v(x)\boldsymbol{\psi}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\right)dx\\
&=\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{h}\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{h}-\int_{I}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}dx_{3}\right)(x):\left(v(x)\boldsymbol{\psi}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\right)\,dx\\
&\quad+\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\int_{I}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}dx_{3}\right)(x)\Big|\partial_{2}\left(\int_{I}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}dx_{3}\right)(x)\Big|0\right):\left(v(x)\boldsymbol{\psi}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\right)dx.\end{split}

(B.12)

From (
B.10
) it follows that

\left\|\int_{I}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\right\|_{L^{2}(\omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{3})}\leq C,\quad\left\|\nabla_{\hat{x}}\int_{I}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\right\|_{L^{2}(\omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{3\times 2})}\leq C,

(B.13)

and

\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\left|\boldsymbol{V}^{h}-\int_{I}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}dx_{3}\right|^{2}\leq\int_{\omega}\int_{I}|\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}|^{2}\leq Ch^{2}.

(B.14)

Note that for
\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{h}:=\boldsymbol{V}^{h}-\int_{I}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}dx_{3}
, we have that
h^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{h}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{h}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
. By using Lemma
B.7
below we conclude that on a subsequence we have that
27
27

27

The additional regularity of
\boldsymbol{\Psi}
follows from the fact that
h^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{h}
and
h^{-1}\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{h}
are bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

h^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{h}\xrightharpoonup{2-r}\boldsymbol{\Psi}(x),\quad\boldsymbol{\Psi}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(I;\mathbb{R}^{3})).

(B.15)

We can write the first integral on the right-hand side in (
B.12
) as follows

\begin{split}&\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{h}(x):\left(v(x)\boldsymbol{\psi}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\right)dx\\
&=\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{h}(x)v(x)\underbrace{\operatorname{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\psi}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)}_{=0}dx+\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{h}}{h}(x)\partial_{3}v(x)\boldsymbol{\psi}^{3}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\,dx\\
&+\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{h}(x)\left(\partial_{1}v(x)\boldsymbol{\psi}^{1}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)+\partial_{2}v(x)\boldsymbol{\psi}^{2}\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\right)dx\\
&=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\Psi}(x)\partial_{3}v(x)\boldsymbol{\psi}^{3}(y)dydx=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\Psi}(x)\partial_{3}v(x)\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{3}(y)\,dy\,dx\\
&=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\Psi}(x)v(x)\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{3}(y)\,dy\,dx.\end{split}

(B.16)

From (
B.13
) it follows that (on a subsequence)
\int_{I}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}dx_{3}\xrightarrow{L^{2}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}},

for some
\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}\in H^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which is as a consequence of (
B.14
) equivalent to
\boldsymbol{V}^{h}\xrightarrow{L^{2}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}
.
By the basic result of two-scale convergence (see
[
2
]
), there exists
\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{Y}};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
such that

\nabla_{\hat{x}}\int_{I}\boldsymbol{V}^{h}dx_{3}\xrightharpoonup{2-r}\nabla_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}+\nabla_{\widehat{y}}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}.

(B.17)

Using the convergences (
B.16
) and (
B.17
) in (
B.12
), we obtain

\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\boldsymbol{V}(x,y):v(x)\boldsymbol{\psi}(y)\,dy\,dx\\
&=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\Psi}(x)v(x)dx\,\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{3}(y)\,dy+\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\nabla_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}+\nabla_{\widehat{y}}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}|0\right):v(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{1}|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{2}|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{3})\,dy\,dx\\
&=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\left[\left(\nabla_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}|\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\Psi}\right)+\left(\nabla_{\widehat{y}}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}|0\right)\right]:v\boldsymbol{\psi}\,dy\,dx.\end{split}

(B.18)

Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\left\{\boldsymbol{V}(x,y)-\left[\left(\nabla_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}|\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\Psi}\right)+\left(\nabla_{\widehat{y}}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}|0\right)\right]\right\}:v\boldsymbol{\psi}\,dy\,dx=0,

\displaystyle\quad\quad\forall v\in C_{c}(\Omega),\ \forall\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})\,\,\text{such that}\,\,\operatorname{div}_{y}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0,

which implies that there exists
\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
such that

\boldsymbol{V}(x,y)-\left[\left(\nabla_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}|\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\Psi}\right)+\left(\nabla_{\widehat{y}}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}|0\right)\right]=\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}.

(B.19)

Finally, the lemma follows directly from (
B.19
) by setting
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}:=\boldsymbol{V}_{2}+\boldsymbol{V}_{3}
,
\boldsymbol{V}_{1}:=\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\Psi}
.
∎

The following lemma was necessary for the proof of Lemma
B.6
. It states that under certain condition we can guarantee that two-scale limit of the sequence doesn’t depend on the fast variable.

Lemma B.7
.

Let
({\varphi}^{h})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
be a bounded sequence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
such that
\varphi^{h}\overset{2-r}{\rightharpoonup}\varphi(x,y)
. Assume that
\varepsilon\nabla_{h}\varphi^{h}\rightarrow 0
strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Then we have that
\varphi(x,y)
depends only on
x
.

Proof.

Let
k\neq 0
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, and
v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
. Without loss of generality we assume
k_{1}\neq 0
. We define

p(y):=\frac{\sin 2\pi ky}{k_{1}}-i\frac{\cos 2\pi ky}{k_{1}}.

Next we have:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{Y}\varphi(x,y)\cdot e^{i2\pi\langle k,y\rangle}v(x)\,dy\,dx

(B.20)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\varphi(x,y)\cdot\partial_{y_{1}}p(y)\cdot v(x)dxdy=\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{h}(x)\cdot\partial_{y_{1}}p\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\cdot v(x)\,dx

(B.21)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{h}(x)\cdot\partial_{1}\left(\varepsilon p\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\cdot v(x)\right)dx-\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{h}(x)\cdot\varepsilon p\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\cdot\partial_{1}v(x)\,dx

(B.22)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{h}(x)\cdot\partial_{1}\left(\varepsilon p\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\cdot v(x)\right)dx=\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\varepsilon\partial_{1}\varphi^{h}(x)\cdot p\left(\frac{\widehat{x}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x_{3}}{\frac{\varepsilon}{h}}\right)\cdot v(x)\,dx=0.

(B.23)

In the analogous way we treat the case when
k_{2}\neq 0
or
k_{3}\neq 0
. Thus we conclude that
\varphi
depends only on
x
.
∎
